s1a

What's the difference between git merge and git rebase, and when should I use each?
baseline
sample 0

Both merge and rebase bring changes from one branch into another. The difference is how they represent that history.

git merge

Combines the histories of two branches and usually creates a merge commit.

git switch feature
git merge main

The feature branch keeps its original commits, and the merge commit records where the histories came together. This preserves the history as it happened, including parallel work.

Use merge when:

  • You want to preserve the shared branch’s history.
  • The branch has already been shared with others.
  • You’re integrating work into a team branch and want an explicit record of the integration.
git rebase

Replays your branch’s commits on top of another branch, producing a linear history. The replayed commits have new IDs.

git switch feature
git rebase main

This makes it look as though your work started from the latest main.

Use rebase when:

  • You want to update a private or local feature branch before merging it.
  • You prefer a clean, linear history.
  • You want to avoid extra merge commits in your project’s history.
Important caution

Rebasing rewrites commit history. Avoid rebasing commits that other people may already have based work on—it can make collaboration confusing and require force-pushing.

A common workflow is to rebase your private feature branch onto the latest main, then merge the feature branch into main when it’s ready.

235 words · 317 tok_est · out 321 · 14.4s · 4.71 overall
cor 5 cla 5 amb 4 sca 5 rel 5 con 5 cmp 4
facts 4/5
sample 1

Both git merge and git rebase bring changes from one branch into another, but they do it differently—and produce different history.

git merge

Combines two branches with a merge commit (unless Git can fast-forward).

git switch main
git merge feature
  • Keeps the original branch history intact.
  • Makes it clear when branches were joined.
  • Is usually the safer choice for shared branches, since it doesn’t rewrite existing commits.
git rebase

Moves your branch’s commits so they appear to start from the tip of another branch.

git switch feature
git rebase main
  • Creates new commit IDs for the rebased commits.
  • Often produces a more linear, easier-to-read history.
  • Rewrites history, so commits that were already shared can cause trouble for collaborators.
Which should I use?
  • Use merge to bring a feature branch into a shared branch, or when you want to preserve the exact history of how work was integrated.
  • Use rebase to update your own local feature branch with the latest changes from main, or to tidy up commits before sharing them.
  • Avoid rebasing commits other people may already have based work on. If you do rebase a published branch, coordinating with collaborators—and often using git push --force-with-lease—is necessary.

A common workflow is to rebase a private feature branch onto the latest main, then merge it when it’s ready.

233 words · 311 tok_est · out 315 · 14.3s · 5.00 overall
cor 5 cla 5 amb 5 sca 5 rel 5 con 5 cmp 5
facts 5/5
sample 2

Both git merge and git rebase integrate changes from one branch into another. The difference is how they represent that integration in history.

git merge: preserve the branch history

If you’re on main and run:

git merge feature

Git combines the branches, usually by creating a merge commit. The history shows that the work happened on separate branches and where they came together.

Use merge when:

  • You want to preserve the full, shared history of a branch.
  • The branch is already used by teammates or pushed for others to rely on.
  • You’re integrating completed work and want a clear record of the integration.
git rebase: replay commits onto a new base

If you’re on feature and run:

git rebase main

Git replays the feature branch’s commits on top of the latest main commits. This creates a linear history, but the replayed commits get new IDs.

Use rebase when:

  • You want a cleaner, linear history.
  • You’re updating your own local feature branch before merging it.
  • You want to tidy up commits before sharing them.
Practical rule
  • Rebase your own, unshared work to keep it current or clean up its history.
  • Merge shared work to avoid rewriting commits that others may already have based their work on.

Rebasing public or shared commits can cause trouble: teammates may have to reconcile their histories. If you do rebase commits that were already pushed, you’ll typically need to push with --force-with-lease, and should coordinate first.

In short: merge preserves history; rebase rewrites it into a linear sequence.

anthropic crisp
sample 0

git merge combines two branches by creating a merge commit (unless it can fast-forward). It preserves the branches’ existing history.

git rebase moves your branch’s commits onto a new base, replaying them one by one. It creates new commit IDs and makes history look linear.

Use merge when you want to preserve the exact shared history, especially on shared branches or when the merge itself is useful context.

Use rebase to tidy your own local commits or update a feature branch from its base before sharing it. Don’t rebase commits other people may already have based work on: rewriting shared history can disrupt their branches.

Example:

Before:  A---B---C   main
              \
               D---E feature

Merge:   A---B---C---M
              \     /
               D---E

Rebase:  A---B---C---D'---E' feature

In short: merge preserves history; rebase rewrites your branch’s history for a linear result.

sample 1

git merge combines two branches by creating a merge commit (unless Git can fast-forward). git rebase moves your branch’s commits onto a new base, replaying them and creating new commit IDs.

Use merge when you want to preserve the branch’s history and record where work joined. It’s a safe default for shared branches because it doesn’t rewrite existing commits.

Use rebase to keep your branch’s history linear, often by updating a local feature branch with the latest target branch before merging it. Rebase rewrites commits, so avoid rebasing commits that others may already have based work on.

Example:

# Merge the target branch into your feature branch
git switch feature
git merge main

# Or replay feature commits on top of the latest main
git switch feature
git rebase main

Both incorporate main into feature; merge records the join, while rebase makes the feature commits appear after main. After a rebase, pushing may require git push --force-with-lease if the branch was already pushed.

Merge: preserve branch history

On main, run:

git merge feature

Git combines the branches, usually with a merge commit. The history shows where the branches split and came together.

openai crisp
sample 0

git merge combines histories; git rebase rewrites one history to place it after another.

Suppose your branch and main have both moved forward:

  • Merge: git merge main joins the two histories, usually with a merge commit. Existing commits keep their IDs. This makes the branch structure visible.
  • Rebase: git rebase main replays your branch’s commits on top of main, creating new commit IDs. The result is usually a straighter, linear history.
When to use each
  • Use merge to integrate shared branches, preserve the record of how work came together, or avoid rewriting commits others may have based work on.
  • Use rebase to update your own local feature branch and keep history linear, especially before merging it into a shared branch.

Rule of thumb: Don’t rebase commits that others may already have pulled or built on; rewriting them can disrupt their work. Rebasing your own unpublished commits is generally fine.

Both approaches can produce the same final files; they differ in how they represent the history.

sample 1

Both merge and rebase bring changes from one branch into another, but they record the project history differently.

  • git merge combines the branches and usually creates a *merge commit*. It preserves the original branch history, including where work split and came back together.
  • git rebase moves your branch’s commits so they appear to start from the latest commit on another branch. This makes history look linear, but rewrites those commits (giving them new IDs).
Example

If you’re working on feature while main advances:

# Merge main into feature
git switch feature
git merge main
# Rebase feature onto main
git switch feature
git rebase main
When to use each

Use merge when:

  • You want to preserve the actual history of how branches came together.
  • The branch is shared with others, or its commits have already been published.
  • You want the safer option that doesn’t rewrite existing commits.

Use rebase when:

  • You want a cleaner, linear history before integrating your work.
  • Your feature branch is private or only you are using it.
  • You’re comfortable resolving conflicts and updating the rewritten branch.

Rule of thumb: Don’t rebase commits other people may already depend on. For a shared branch, merge is usually safer. After rebasing a branch you’ve pushed, updating the remote may require git push --force-with-lease; use that carefully.

Suppose your branch and main have both moved forward:

  • You’re integrating a shared branch and want to preserve its history.
  • You don’t want to rewrite commits others may already have based work on.
  • You want the merge itself recorded.
git switch my-branch
git merge main
Use rebase when
  • You’re updating your own local feature branch and want a linear history.
  • You’re comfortable resolving conflicts as commits are replayed.
git switch my-branch
git rebase main

Rule of thumb: Rebase your own unpublished work; merge shared or published work. Rebasing changes commit IDs, so avoid rebasing commits that teammates may already have pulled. After rebasing a branch you’ve pushed, updating the remote usually requires git push --force-with-lease—use that only when appropriate.

s1b

In Python, what's the difference between `is` and `==`? When does it matter?
baseline
sample 0

== checks whether two values are equal. is checks whether two references point to the same object.

a = [1, 2]
b = [1, 2]

a == b  # True: the lists have equal contents
a is b  # False: they are different list objects

They can both be true when two names refer to the same object:

a = [1, 2]
b = a

a == b  # True
a is b  # True

Use == for ordinary value comparisons. Use is when object identity matters—most commonly to check for None:

if result is None:
    ...

Don’t use is to compare numbers, strings, or other values. Python may reuse some objects internally, so identity results can be surprising and aren’t a substitute for equality.

131 words · 193 tok_est · out 197 · 6.2s · 4.57 overall
cor 5 cla 5 amb 4 sca 5 rel 5 con 5 cmp 3
facts 2/5
sample 1

== checks equality: whether two values are considered equivalent.

is checks identity: whether two names refer to the exact same object.

a = [1, 2]
b = [1, 2]

a == b  # True: same contents
a is b  # False: distinct list objects

It matters when you need to know whether something is the *same object*, not just an equal one—for example, with singletons such as None:

if value is None:
    ...

Use == for ordinary value comparisons. Use is mainly for None (and other intentional singleton checks). Don’t rely on is to compare strings or numbers: Python may reuse some objects, so identity results can vary.

112 words · 170 tok_est · out 174 · 5.8s · 4.86 overall
cor 5 cla 5 amb 5 sca 5 rel 5 con 5 cmp 4
facts 4/5
sample 2

== asks whether two objects have equal values.
is asks whether they are the same object in memory.

a = [1, 2]
b = [1, 2]

a == b  # True: same contents
a is b  # False: distinct list objects

It matters when you need to check identity, most commonly against None:

if result is None:
    ...

Use == for ordinary value comparisons, such as strings, numbers, and lists. Use is when you specifically mean “this exact object”—for example, checking a sentinel object:

MISSING = object()

if value is MISSING:
    ...

Don’t use is to compare strings or numbers. Python may reuse some objects internally, so identity can appear to work in some cases and fail in others. For example, use x == 0, not x is 0.

anthropic crisp
sample 0

== checks whether two objects have equal values. is checks whether they are the same object.

a = [1, 2]
b = [1, 2]

a == b  # True: same contents
a is b  # False: separate list objects

It matters when you need to check identity, not value—for example, whether a variable is exactly None:

if result is None:
    ...

Use == for ordinary value comparisons. Use is for identity checks, commonly with None, True, or False when identity is specifically intended. Don’t use is to compare strings or numbers: whether they happen to be the same object can vary with Python’s implementation and how they were created.

sample 1

== checks whether two objects have equal values. is checks whether they are the same object.

a = [1, 2]
b = [1, 2]

a == b  # True: same contents
a is b  # False: different list objects

c = a
a is c  # True: c refers to the same object as a

Use == for value comparisons. Use is when identity matters—most commonly to check for None:

if result is None:
    ...

Don’t use is to compare strings, numbers, or other values. Python may reuse objects in some cases, so a is b can appear true for equal values and change across runs or implementations.

s2a

This Python prints ['a'] then ['a', 'b'], but I expected ['a'] then ['b']. Why, and how do I fix it? ```python def add_item(item, items=[]): items.append(item) return items print(add_item("a")) print(add_item("b")) ```
baseline
sample 0

Default argument values are created once, when Python defines the function—not each time it’s called. Both calls therefore use the same list, so the second call appends "b" to the list that already contains "a".

Use None as the default and create a new list inside the function:

def add_item(item, items=None):
    if items is None:
        items = []
    items.append(item)
    return items

print(add_item("a"))  # ['a']
print(add_item("b"))  # ['b']

This pattern also lets callers pass in an existing list when they want to append to it.

s2b

I get `RuntimeError: dictionary changed size during iteration` from this. How do I fix it? ```python counts = {"a": 0, "b": 2, "c": 0} for k in counts: if counts[k] == 0: del counts[k] ```
baseline
sample 0

Deleting a key changes the dictionary while Python is iterating over it. Iterate over a snapshot of the keys instead:

for k in list(counts):
    if counts[k] == 0:
        del counts[k]

Or, more simply, build a new dictionary:

counts = {k: v for k, v in counts.items() if v != 0}

Or, more concisely, build a new dictionary:

counts = {k: v for k, v in counts.items() if v != 0}

Deleting a key while iterating over a dictionary changes it during iteration. Iterate over a snapshot of the keys:

for k in list(counts):
    if counts[k] == 0:
        del counts[k]

Or create a new dictionary without zero values:

counts = {k: v for k, v in counts.items() if v != 0}

s3a

Write an implementation plan for adding per-user rate limiting to an existing Express API: 100 requests per minute per user, Redis-backed, and over-limit requests get a 429 with a Retry-After header. The API already has a JWT middleware that sets req.user.id. A coding agent will execute the plan.
baseline
sample 0
Implementation plan
1. Inspect the existing API setup
  • Find where Express middleware is registered, how routes are mounted, and how errors/responses are formatted.
  • Identify the Redis client and its conventions for connection, key naming, and error handling. Reuse existing infrastructure; do not create a second client.
  • Confirm that JWT middleware sets req.user.id and runs before the routes to be limited.
  • Check the project’s test framework and dependency versions.
2. Add a Redis-backed per-user limiter
  • Implement a limiter middleware (or configure the project’s existing rate-limit package) with a 100-request limit per user per rolling 60-second window.
  • Use req.user.id as the identity. Ensure the Redis key includes a stable namespace, such as rate-limit:user:<id>, so it cannot collide with unrelated keys.
  • Make counting and expiration atomic across application instances—for example, use a Redis Lua script that increments the key and sets its expiry on the first request. Avoid separate, race-prone increment and expiry operations.
  • Define and document the exact counting behavior: the first 100 requests in the window are allowed; request 101 is rejected.
  • Calculate the wait until the window resets and return it as an integer number of seconds, rounded up to at least 1.
3. Register middleware in the correct order
  • Register the limiter after JWT authentication has populated req.user.id and before the protected routes it should cover.
  • Apply it to the intended API routes consistently. Do not accidentally limit health checks or public routes unless that is desired.
  • Decide how to handle requests reaching the limiter without a user ID: authentication should normally reject these first; if not, fail safely rather than treating all such requests as one user.
4. Return the limit response
  • For requests over the limit, respond with HTTP 429 Too Many Requests.
  • Include Retry-After with the calculated number of seconds until the user can make another request.
  • Match the API’s existing response format, for example a JSON error body with a stable code such as RATE_LIMIT_EXCEEDED.
  • Do not invoke downstream route handlers for rejected requests.
5. Handle Redis failures deliberately
  • Follow the project’s availability policy for Redis outages. Choose and document whether to fail closed (reject requests) or fail open (allow requests), and make the behavior explicit in code.
  • Log or report Redis errors without exposing internal details in API responses.
6. Add tests
  • Unit-test the limiter’s boundary behavior: requests 1–100 pass, request 101 returns 429, and the rejection includes a valid Retry-After.
  • Verify separate user IDs have independent limits.
  • Verify the window expires and a user can make requests again afterward.
  • Verify the middleware runs after authentication and before protected handlers.
  • Test the chosen Redis-failure behavior and the response format.
  • Use a real Redis test instance or a suitable Redis test setup to verify atomic counting and expiry semantics.
7. Validate and document
  • Run the relevant test suite, linting, and type checks.
  • Document the limit, window semantics, Redis dependency, and failure policy in the API or operations documentation.
  • Ensure deployment configuration provides the Redis connection settings required by the existing client.

Implementation plan
  • Inspect the existing API
  • Locate the Express app setup, JWT authentication middleware, route registration, and error-handling middleware.
  • Check the existing Redis client setup and conventions for configuration, logging, and tests.
  • Confirm that protected routes run after the JWT middleware and that req.user.id is available there.
  • Add or reuse a Redis client
  • Reuse the application’s shared Redis client if one exists. Otherwise, create one using the project’s existing configuration and lifecycle conventions.
  • Read the Redis connection settings from environment/configuration; do not hard-code credentials.
  • Ensure startup handles Redis connection failures according to the project’s operational policy. Do not silently fall back to unbounded traffic if Redis is unavailable.
  • Implement per-user rate-limit middleware
  • Add middleware that uses req.user.id as the rate-limit key, namespaced to avoid collisions (for example, rate-limit:user:<id>).
  • Use a fixed 60-second window with a maximum of 100 requests per user. Make the increment and window assignment atomic in Redis, using a Lua script or an equivalent atomic Redis operation. Ensure the key receives a 60-second expiry when its counter is first created.
  • Define whether requests 1–100 are allowed and request 101 is rejected. Apply the same rule consistently.
  • If req.user.id is missing, do not create a shared “undefined user” bucket. Treat this as an authentication/ordering error consistent with the API’s conventions.
  • Return the required response when limited
  • When the count exceeds 100, return HTTP 429 Too Many Requests and do not call next().
  • Set Retry-After to the number of whole seconds remaining in the current window, rounded up to at least 1.
  • Use the API’s existing error-response format where possible, with a clear rate-limit message.
  • Optionally include standard limit/remaining/reset headers if consistent with existing API conventions; Retry-After is required.
  • Apply middleware in the correct order
  • Mount the limiter after JWT authentication so it can use req.user.id.
  • Apply it to the intended protected API routes. Avoid accidentally limiting public routes or applying it twice through overlapping mounts.
  • Confirm that all routes intended to share a user quota use the same Redis key namespace and window.
  • Handle Redis errors deliberately
  • Follow the service’s established dependency-failure policy. If no policy exists, fail closed with a service-unavailable response rather than bypassing the limit.
  • Log Redis failures without logging tokens or other sensitive user data.
  • Ensure an error is not mistakenly returned as a 429; 429 should represent an exceeded quota only.
  • Add tests
  • Unit-test the limiter for counts below, at, and above the 100-request limit.
  • Verify that different user IDs have independent counters.
  • Verify Retry-After is present, positive, and based on the remaining window when a request is rejected.
  • Test that the counter expires after 60 seconds and that concurrent increments cannot exceed the limit incorrectly.
  • Test middleware ordering/behavior with authenticated and missing-user requests, plus the chosen Redis-failure behavior.
  • Use a real Redis test instance or a suitable test Redis implementation for atomicity and expiry behavior; avoid relying solely on mocks for those guarantees.
  • Document and validate
  • Document any new Redis configuration and the rate-limit behavior.
  • Run the relevant unit/integration tests, linting, and type checks.
  • Verify a local or staging sequence: 100 requests for one user are accepted, the 101st receives 429 with Retry-After, and another user remains unaffected.

sample 2

Implementation plan: per-user Redis rate limiting

Goal

Limit authenticated users to 100 requests per rolling 60-second window. Identify users using req.user.id, store counters in Redis, and respond to excess requests with HTTP 429 and a Retry-After header.

1. Inspect the existing API
  • Find the Express app setup, JWT authentication middleware, route registration, and error-handling middleware.
  • Check how Redis is configured and accessed. Reuse the existing client and configuration if available; otherwise, add Redis configuration following the project’s conventions.
  • Identify which routes require authentication and where middleware runs relative to route handlers.
  • Check existing rate-limiting packages and test patterns before adding dependencies.
2. Implement the limiter

Add a reusable Express middleware that:

  • Reads the authenticated user ID from req.user.id. Mount it after JWT authentication on the routes it should protect.
  • Uses a Redis key scoped to the user and limiter, such as rate-limit:user:<userId>.
  • Atomically increments the counter and sets its expiration to 60 seconds on the first request. Use a Redis operation or script that avoids races between incrementing and setting the expiry.
  • Allows requests while the count is at most 100.
  • For requests above 100, responds without calling next():
  • Status: 429
  • JSON body using the project’s standard error format
  • Retry-After: integer seconds until the current window expires, rounded up to at least 1
  • Sets the Retry-After header only on rejected requests unless project conventions specify otherwise.

Choose and document the intended window semantics. A fixed window starting with the user’s first request is suitable for this implementation; do not accidentally reset the window on every request.

3. Handle Redis failures deliberately

Use the API’s existing Redis failure policy if one exists. Otherwise, make the behavior explicit in code and tests. For an API availability-oriented default, log Redis errors and allow the request through; do not silently treat Redis failures as an exceeded limit. Keep error handling compatible with the Redis client version and Express version in use.

4. Mount middleware
  • Apply the limiter after JWT authentication so req.user.id is present.
  • Apply it only to authenticated routes covered by the requirement. Avoid accidentally limiting public endpoints or counting unauthenticated requests as one shared user.
  • Confirm that requests rejected by the limiter do not reach route handlers.
5. Tests

Add or update tests covering:

  • Requests 1 through 100 for a user are allowed; request 101 is rejected with status 429 and a valid Retry-After header.
  • The counter expires after 60 seconds and the user can make requests again.
  • Two different user IDs have independent limits.
  • The limiter runs after authentication and uses req.user.id.
  • Rejected requests do not invoke downstream handlers.
  • Redis errors follow the chosen failure policy.
  • The Retry-After value is an integer of at least 1 and reflects the remaining window time.

Use a mocked Redis client or the project’s integration-test Redis setup, consistent with existing tests.

6. Verify
  • Run the relevant unit and integration tests, then the project’s full test and lint/type-check commands.
  • Confirm the implementation uses atomic counter/expiry behavior with the installed Redis client.
  • Document the configured limit and Redis dependency in the project’s usual configuration or operational documentation.

  • Build Redis keys with a dedicated namespace and the authenticated user ID, such as rate-limit:user:<id>:<window-start>. Align the window start to a minute boundary so all API instances use the same window.
  • Increment the key atomically. Set its 60-second expiry only when it is first created; use a Lua script or equivalent atomic Redis operation to avoid races between incrementing and setting expiry.
  • If req.user.id is missing, do not create a shared or anonymous user bucket. Follow the API’s existing authentication behavior and ensure this middleware is not used in place of authentication.
  • On Redis errors, follow the service’s established policy. Do not silently allow unlimited requests unless that is the intended availability policy; log the failure without exposing Redis details to clients.
  • Return the rate-limit response
  • Allow requests while the count is at most 100.
  • For counts above 100, return HTTP 429 Too Many Requests and do not call next().
  • Set Retry-After to the number of seconds until the current minute window resets, rounded up and never less than 1.
  • If the API already defines an error response format, use it. Otherwise return a small JSON error body, for example {"error":"Too many requests"}.
  • Register the middleware
  • Apply it to the intended authenticated routes, after JWT middleware and before their handlers.
  • Verify that route groups and mounted routers preserve this ordering. Do not apply it to excluded routes.

Implementation plan: per-user Redis rate limiting

Goal

Limit authenticated users to 100 requests per rolling 60-second window, keyed by req.user.id. Store counters in Redis. Reject excess requests with HTTP 429 and a Retry-After header.

1. Inspect the API
  • Find the Express app setup, JWT authentication middleware, route registration, and error handlers.
  • Check how Redis is configured and accessed. Reuse the existing client and configuration; otherwise, follow project conventions to add them.
  • Identify authenticated routes and middleware order.
  • Check existing rate-limiting dependencies and test patterns before adding packages.
2. Implement the limiter

Add reusable Express middleware that:

  • Reads req.user.id and runs after JWT authentication.
  • Uses a key scoped to the user and limiter, such as rate-limit:user:<userId>.
  • Atomically increments the counter and sets a 60-second expiry on the first request. Use a Redis operation or script that prevents races between incrementing and setting the expiry.
  • Allows requests when the count is at most 100.
  • Rejects requests above 100 without calling next():
  • Return HTTP 429.
  • Use the project’s standard JSON error format.
  • Set Retry-After to the remaining window in integer seconds, rounded up to at least 1.
  • Sets Retry-After only on rejected requests, unless project conventions specify otherwise.

Use a fixed window that starts with the user’s first request. Do not reset the window on every request.

3. Handle Redis failures

Use the API’s existing Redis failure policy. If none exists, define the behavior in code and tests. For an availability-oriented default, log Redis errors and allow the request through; do not treat Redis failures as limit violations. Keep error handling compatible with the installed Redis client and Express versions.

4. Mount the middleware
  • Mount the limiter after JWT authentication so req.user.id is available.
  • Apply it only to authenticated routes covered by the requirement. Do not limit public endpoints or count unauthenticated requests under a shared user.
  • Confirm rejected requests do not reach route handlers.
5. Test

Add or update tests to verify:

  • Requests 1–100 are allowed; request 101 returns 429 with a valid Retry-After.
  • The counter expires after 60 seconds, allowing requests again.
  • Different user IDs have independent limits.
  • The limiter runs after authentication and uses req.user.id.
  • Rejected requests do not invoke downstream handlers.
  • Redis errors follow the chosen failure policy.
  • Retry-After is an integer of at least 1 and reflects the remaining window.

Use a mocked Redis client or the project’s integration-test Redis setup, consistent with existing tests.

6. Verify
  • Run relevant unit and integration tests, then the full test suite and lint/type checks.
  • Confirm the installed Redis client supports the chosen atomic counter-and-expiry operation.
  • Document the limit and Redis dependency in the project’s standard configuration or operations documentation.

Goal

s3b

Write a spec for a small CLI tool called `envcheck`. It compares a `.env.example` file against a `.env` file and reports keys that are missing from `.env`, keys that are extra in `.env`, and keys that are present but empty. Exit code 0 if nothing is missing, 1 otherwise. A coding agent will implement it from your spec.
baseline
sample 0

envcheck specification

Purpose

envcheck compares the variable names in .env.example with those in .env. It reports:

  • Missing keys: listed in .env.example but not in .env
  • Extra keys: listed in .env but not in .env.example
  • Empty keys: present in .env with an empty value
Invocation
envcheck [--example PATH] [--env PATH]

Defaults:

  • --example: .env.example
  • --env: .env

Paths are relative to the current working directory unless absolute.

Input parsing

Read each file as UTF-8 text, one assignment per line. Ignore blank lines and lines whose first non-whitespace character is #.

For other lines:

  • Trim leading and trailing whitespace.
  • If the line begins with export , remove that prefix and trim again.
  • Split at the first =.
  • The trimmed text before = is the key; the text after it is the value.
  • Ignore lines with no = or an empty key.

Compare keys exactly and case-sensitively. Do not expand variables, interpret quotes, or otherwise transform values. A value is empty if it is empty after trimming whitespace; thus KEY=, KEY= , and KEY="" count as empty, while KEY=" " does not.

If a key appears more than once in a file, treat it as present once. For the .env file, consider the key empty if any of its assignments has an empty value.

Output

Print each non-empty category to standard output, in this order:

Missing keys:
  KEY

Extra keys:
  KEY

Empty keys:
  KEY

Sort keys lexicographically within each category. Omit categories with no keys. If there are no findings, print:

No issues found.
Exit status
  • 0 if no keys are missing, including when there are extra or empty keys.
  • 1 if one or more keys are missing.
  • For file-read or other operational errors, print a useful message to standard error and exit with a nonzero status other than 0. Do not report such errors as ordinary key findings.
329 words · 484 tok_est · out 488 · 12.5s · 4.14 overall
cor 3 cla 4 amb 3 sca 5 rel 5 con 5 cmp 4
facts 5/6
sample 1

envcheck CLI specification

Purpose

envcheck compares the variable names declared in .env.example with those in .env. It reports:

  • Missing keys: present in .env.example but absent from .env
  • Extra keys: present in .env but absent from .env.example
  • Empty keys: present in .env with an empty value
Invocation
envcheck [--example PATH] [--env PATH]

Defaults:

  • --example: .env.example
  • --env: .env

Paths are resolved relative to the current working directory.

Input parsing

Read both files as UTF-8 text. A line defines a key when, after trimming leading whitespace, it is not blank or a comment and contains an assignment of the form KEY=VALUE.

  • Ignore blank lines and lines whose first non-whitespace character is #.
  • Split an assignment at its first =.
  • Trim whitespace around the key. The key must be non-empty; ignore lines with an empty key.
  • Do not interpret quoting, escapes, interpolation, or inline comments. The value is everything after the first =.
  • Treat a key as empty when its value is empty after trimming whitespace. Thus KEY=, KEY= , and KEY= # comment are empty; KEY=" " is not empty.
  • If a key occurs more than once in a file, treat it as one key. For .env empty-key reporting, consider the key empty if its last assignment has an empty value.
Output

Write a section for each non-empty result category to standard output, in this order:

Missing keys:
  KEY

Extra keys:
  KEY

Empty keys:
  KEY

List keys alphabetically within each section. Omit sections with no entries. If there are no missing, extra, or empty keys, print:

No issues found.
Exit status
  • 0 when there are no missing keys.
  • 1 when one or more keys are missing.

Extra or empty keys alone do not change the exit status.

Errors

If either file cannot be read, or is not valid UTF-8, print a concise error to standard error and exit with a nonzero status other than 0. Do not print a comparison report when input loading fails.

envcheck Specification

Purpose

envcheck compares the variable names in .env.example with those in .env. It reports:

  • Missing keys: listed in .env.example, but not in .env.
  • Extra keys: listed in .env, but not in .env.example.
  • Empty keys: listed in .env with an empty value.
Invocation
envcheck [--example PATH] [--env PATH]

Defaults:

  • --example: .env.example
  • --env: .env

Paths are relative to the current working directory unless absolute. Do not modify either file.

Parsing

Read files as UTF-8, one logical entry per line.

  • Ignore blank lines and lines whose first non-whitespace character is #.
  • For other lines, use the text before the first = as the key, trimming surrounding whitespace.
  • Lines without = are ignored.
  • A key is empty or invalid if it is blank after trimming; ignore such lines.
  • For .env, the value is the text after the first =, with surrounding whitespace trimmed. A value is empty if that result is empty. Quotes are not stripped: KEY="" is not empty.
  • Compare keys exactly and case-sensitively.
  • If a key appears more than once in a file, treat it as one key. For .env, it is empty if any occurrence has an empty value.

Example:

Missing keys:
  API_URL
Extra keys:
  DEBUG
Empty keys:
  TOKEN

If there are no missing, extra, or empty keys, print:

All environment keys are present and non-empty.
Exit codes
  • 0: No keys are missing from .env. Extra or empty keys alone do not change the exit code.
  • 1: One or more keys from .env.example are missing from .env.
  • 2: A file cannot be read, or command-line arguments are invalid. Print a diagnostic to standard error.

A successfully read empty file is valid and contains no keys.

envcheck specification

Purpose

envcheck compares variable names in .env.example with those in .env. It reports:

  • Missing: keys in .env.example but not in .env.
  • Extra: keys in .env but not in .env.example.
  • Empty: keys in .env whose value is empty.

Exit code is 1 if any keys are missing; otherwise it is 0. Extra or empty keys alone do not affect the exit code.

Command
envcheck [--example PATH] [--env PATH]

Defaults:

  • --example: .env.example
  • --env: .env

Paths are relative to the current working directory unless absolute.

Input format

Read each file as UTF-8 text, one line at a time.

Recognize assignments in this form:

KEY=value

A key must match [A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]*. Allow whitespace before and after the key and around =. The value is everything after =, with leading and trailing whitespace removed.

Ignore blank lines and lines whose first non-whitespace character is #. Ignore lines that do not match the assignment form.

Do not expand variables, remove quotes, or interpret inline comments. For example, KEY="" has the value "" and is not empty; KEY= is empty.

If a key appears more than once in a file, use its last assignment. For empty-key reporting, the last assignment in .env determines whether that key is empty.

Output

Write reports to standard output, in this order, omitting sections with no entries:

Missing keys:
  KEY

Extra keys:
  KEY

Empty keys:
  KEY

Sort keys alphabetically within each section. If there are no missing, extra, or empty keys, print:

Environment is valid.
Errors

If either file cannot be read, print an error naming the path to standard error and exit with code 2. Do not print a normal report when an input file cannot be read. Do not create or modify files.

Exit codes
  • 0: no missing keys; extra or empty keys may still be reported.
  • 1: one or more missing keys.
  • 2: input file error.

envcheck specification

Purpose

envcheck compares variable names in .env.example with those in .env. It reports:

  • Missing: in .env.example, but not in .env.
  • Extra: in .env, but not in .env.example.
  • Empty: in both files, but set to an empty value in .env.
Command line
envcheck [--example PATH] [--env PATH]

Defaults:

  • --example: .env.example
  • --env: .env

Paths are relative to the current working directory unless absolute. Each option may appear at most once. An unknown option, a missing option value, or a repeated option is a usage error: print an error to stderr and exit 2.

Parsing

Read both files as UTF-8 text. If either file cannot be read or decoded, print the path and error to stderr and exit 2.

For each line:

  • Remove leading and trailing whitespace.
  • Ignore the line if it is empty or starts with #.
  • If the line starts with export , remove that prefix and trim whitespace again.
  • Split at the first =.
  • Use the trimmed text before = as the key. Ignore lines with no = or an empty key.
  • Treat everything after the first = as the value and trim surrounding whitespace.

Values are not expanded, unquoted, or interpreted as shell syntax. A value is empty when its trimmed text is empty; for example, KEY=, KEY= , and KEY="" are not all equivalent: the first two are empty, while KEY="" is nonempty.

If a key appears more than once in a file, treat it as one key. For .env, the key is empty if any occurrence has an empty value.

Comparison and output

Compare keys exactly and case-sensitively.

Write results to stdout in this order, omitting any category with no entries:

Missing in .env: KEY_A, KEY_B
Extra in .env: KEY_C
Empty in .env: KEY_D

Sort keys lexicographically within each category. If there are no missing, extra, or empty keys, print:

.env matches .env.example

Do not print values.

Exit status
  • 0: no missing keys. Extra or empty keys do not change this status.
  • 1: one or more keys are missing from .env.
  • 2: usage, file-read, or file-decoding error.

envcheck specification

Purpose

envcheck compares the keys in .env.example with the keys in .env. It reports:

  • Missing: keys in .env.example but not in .env.
  • Extra: keys in .env but not in .env.example.
  • Empty: keys in .env whose parsed value is empty.

Exit status depends only on missing keys.

Invocation
envcheck [--example PATH] [--env PATH]

Defaults:

  • --example: .env.example
  • --env: .env

Paths are relative to the current working directory. Options may appear in either order. An option without a following path is a usage error.

File parsing

Read both files as UTF-8 text. Parse each nonblank line as follows:

  • Ignore leading whitespace.
  • Ignore the line if it is blank or its first non-whitespace character is #.
  • If the line starts with export , remove that prefix.
  • Split at the first =.
  • Trim whitespace around the key. The key is the text before =.
  • Treat the text after = as the value and trim whitespace around it.
  • Remove one matching pair of surrounding single or double quotes from the value.
  • Record the key and parsed value.

Lines without = are ignored. Do not expand variables, process escape sequences, or interpret inline comments. If a key appears more than once, use its last value. A repeated key counts as one key.

Output

On success, print one line for each nonempty result category, in this order:

Missing: KEY1, KEY2
Extra: KEY3
Empty: KEY4, KEY5

Within each category, sort keys in bytewise ascending order. Omit categories with no keys. If all categories are empty, print:

No issues found.

Write diagnostics to standard error, not standard output.

Exit status and errors
  • Exit 0 when no keys are missing, even if extra or empty keys exist.
  • Exit 1 when one or more keys are missing.
  • Exit 2 if an input file cannot be read, is not valid UTF-8, or the command line is invalid. Print a concise diagnostic to standard error; do not print a comparison report.
  • If both files contain errors, report the first error encountered, checking .env.example before .env.

envcheck specification

Purpose

envcheck compares the variable names in a .env.example file with those in a .env file. It reports:

  • Missing keys: in .env.example but not in .env
  • Extra keys: in .env but not in .env.example
  • Empty keys: in .env with an empty value
Invocation
envcheck [--example PATH] [--env PATH]

Defaults:

  • --example: .env.example
  • --env: .env

Paths are relative to the current working directory unless absolute. The tool reads files only; it does not modify them.

Parsing

For each file:

  • Read it as UTF-8 text, allowing a final line without a newline.
  • Process each line independently.
  • Ignore blank lines and lines whose first non-whitespace character is #.
  • Ignore an optional export prefix, followed by optional whitespace.
  • For other lines, use the text before the first = as the key, trimming surrounding whitespace. Lines without = do not define a key and are ignored.
  • Treat keys as case-sensitive. Compare keys exactly after trimming.
  • For .env, a key is empty if the text after the first = is empty or consists only of whitespace. Do not treat quotes or comments specially: for example, KEY="" is non-empty.

If a key appears more than once in a file, treat it as one key for comparisons. For empty-key reporting, report a key if any of its assignments in .env has an empty value.

Output

On successful parsing, print one line for each non-empty result category, in this order:

Missing: KEY1, KEY2
Extra: KEY3
Empty: KEY4

Within each category, sort keys lexicographically. Omit categories with no results. If there are no results, print:

All environment keys are present and non-empty.

Write reports to standard output.

Exit status
  • Exit 0 if there are no missing keys.
  • Exit 1 if one or more keys are missing.
  • Extra or empty keys alone do not change the exit status.
  • If a file cannot be read or decoded, print a concise error to standard error and exit with a nonzero status other than 0 or 1 (use 2).
Examples

Given .env.example:

API_KEY=
PORT=3000

and .env:

API_KEY=secret
DEBUG=true

Output:

Extra: DEBUG

Exit status: 0.

If .env instead omits PORT:

Missing: PORT
Extra: DEBUG

Exit status: 1.

envcheck CLI specification

Purpose

envcheck compares the keys in a .env.example file with those in a .env file. It reports:

  • Missing keys: in .env.example but not .env.
  • Extra keys: in .env but not .env.example.
  • Empty keys: present in .env with an empty value.
Invocation
envcheck [--example PATH] [--env PATH]

Defaults:

  • --example: .env.example
  • --env: .env

Paths are relative to the current working directory unless absolute. Each option may be specified at most once. Unknown options or missing option values are usage errors.

File and parsing rules
  • Read both files as UTF-8 text. If either file cannot be read or decoded, report an error to stderr and exit nonzero.
  • Ignore blank lines and lines whose first non-whitespace character is #.
  • For each other line, use the first = to separate the key from the value. Trim whitespace around the key. Treat the remainder of the line as the value, trimming surrounding whitespace.
  • Lines without = or with an empty key are invalid. Report the file and line number to stderr; continue parsing so all invalid lines can be reported.
  • Do not interpret shell syntax, expand variables, or remove quotes. For example, KEY="" has the nonempty value ""; KEY= has an empty value.
  • If a key appears more than once in a file, report it as a parse error. Do not count duplicate occurrences as additional keys.
Output

On success, write a report to stdout. List each category in this order: Missing keys, Extra keys, Empty keys. Sort keys alphabetically within each category. Show (none) for an empty category.

  API_SECRET
Extra keys:
  DEBUG
Empty keys:
  DATABASE_URL

If there are no findings:

Missing keys:
  (none)
Extra keys:
  (none)
Empty keys:
  (none)
Exit status
  • 0 if parsing succeeds and no keys are missing. Extra or empty keys do not affect the exit status.
  • 1 if one or more keys are missing.
  • Use a separate nonzero status for usage, file, decoding, or parse errors. Do not report a successful comparison when an input has such an error.

envcheck specification

Purpose

envcheck compares the variable names in a .env.example file with those in a .env file. It reports:

  • Missing keys: present in .env.example, absent from .env.
  • Extra keys: present in .env, absent from .env.example.
  • Empty keys: present in both files, but with an empty value in .env.
Command-line interface
envcheck [--example PATH] [--env PATH]
  • --example PATH: path to the template file. Default: .env.example.
  • --env PATH: path to the environment file. Default: .env.
  • --help: print usage and exit successfully.

Paths are interpreted relative to the current working directory.

File parsing

Parse each file as UTF-8 text, one line at a time.

  • Ignore blank lines and lines whose first non-whitespace character is #.
  • For other lines, use the text before the first = as the key, and the text after it as the value.
  • Trim whitespace around the key. Do not trim the value when deciding whether it is empty.
  • A line without = has a key and no value. In .env.example, this declares a required key. In .env, it is present and empty.
  • Ignore lines whose parsed key is empty.
  • If a key appears more than once in a file, treat it as one key. For .env, use its last occurrence to determine whether its value is empty.
  • Do not expand variables, remove quotes, or interpret escape sequences.

A value is empty if it is absent or consists of zero characters. A value containing spaces is not empty.

Output

Print each non-empty category to standard output, in this order: missing, extra, empty. Sort keys alphabetically within each category.

Use this format:

Missing keys: KEY_A, KEY_B
Extra keys: KEY_C
Empty keys: KEY_D

Omit a category line when it has no keys. If there are no findings, print:

No issues found.
Exit codes
  • 0: no keys are missing from .env. Extra or empty keys do not change the exit code.
  • 1: one or more keys are missing from .env.
  • 2: an operational error occurred, such as an input file not existing, being unreadable, or containing invalid UTF-8. Print a useful error message to standard error.

sample 2

envcheck Specification

Purpose

envcheck compares the variable names in .env.example and .env, reporting:

  • Missing keys: in .env.example but not .env.
  • Extra keys: in .env but not .env.example.
  • Empty keys: in .env with an empty value.
Invocation
envcheck [--example PATH] [--env PATH]

Defaults: --example is .env.example; --env is .env. Paths are relative to the current working directory unless absolute. Neither file is modified.

Parsing

Read files as UTF-8, with one logical entry per line.

  • Ignore blank lines and lines whose first non-whitespace character is #.
  • Ignore lines without =.
  • For other lines, take the text before the first = as the key and trim surrounding whitespace. Ignore keys that are blank after trimming.
  • In .env, take the text after the first = as the value and trim surrounding whitespace. The value is empty if nothing remains. Do not strip quotes: KEY="" is not empty.
  • Compare keys exactly and case-sensitively. Treat duplicate keys as one key; in .env, a key is empty if any occurrence has an empty value.
Output

Print a summary to standard output. List keys alphabetically within each category, one per line, and omit categories with no keys.

Missing keys:
  API_URL
Extra keys:
  DEBUG
Empty keys:
  TOKEN

If there are no missing, extra, or empty keys, print:

All environment keys are present and non-empty.
Exit codes
  • 0: No keys are missing from .env. Extra or empty keys do not affect the exit code.
  • 1: One or more keys from .env.example are missing from .env.
  • 2: A file cannot be read or command-line arguments are invalid. Print a diagnostic to standard error.

A successfully read empty file is valid and contains no keys.

Incident update — v2.14

v2.14 deployed at 14:05. At 14:12, errors on /api/orders rose from 0.2% to 6%. We rolled back at 14:20, and error rates returned to normal by 14:22.

The new orders query referenced a column missing from the EU replica because the migration had only run on US. About 400 order submissions failed; customers saw a generic error. No customers were double-charged, as payment occurs after order creation.

Next steps: Priya to confirm the EU migration is complete tonight. We’ll add a deployment check to verify migrations have run on all replicas before app deployment. Re-deploy is planned for tomorrow at 10:00, after the migration.

s5a

Compare Postgres LISTEN/NOTIFY, Redis Pub/Sub, and Kafka for a notification service handling about 1,000 events per second, where losing an occasional event is acceptable and we already run Postgres and Redis. Recommend one.
baseline
sample 0

Recommendation: use Redis Pub/Sub for this service, assuming notifications are transient and subscribers can tolerate missing events.

At roughly 1,000 events/second, all three options can handle the rate in a suitably provisioned setup. The deciding factor is the delivery guarantee and operational cost:

| Option | Delivery behavior | Fit here |
|---|---|---|
| Postgres LISTEN/NOTIFY | Ephemeral: listeners that are disconnected miss notifications. Notifications are delivered after the transaction commits, and payloads are limited in size. | Viable for modest, database-adjacent signaling, but makes Postgres do extra work and couples notification traffic to the database. |
| Redis Pub/Sub | Ephemeral: subscribers disconnected at publish time miss messages. Low-latency fan-out with little setup. | Best fit: Redis is already available, and occasional loss is acceptable. |
| Kafka | Durable, replayable log with consumer offsets and stronger recovery options. | More capability and operational overhead than you need if losing occasional events is acceptable. |

Use Redis Pub/Sub if the messages are best-effort signals—for example, “refresh this view”—rather than the only record of important business actions. Keep authoritative data in Postgres, and have consumers recover or refresh state when they reconnect.

If you later need replay, reliable processing, or per-consumer delivery tracking, switch to a durable mechanism such as Kafka, or a Redis Streams-based design.

216 words · 302 tok_est · out 306 · 13.4s · 4.57 overall
cor 5 cla 5 amb 4 sca 5 rel 5 con 5 cmp 3
facts 3/5
sample 1

Recommendation: use Postgres LISTEN/NOTIFY, assuming notifications are transient signals and the service can recover by checking its source of truth. At roughly 1,000 events/second, it’s a reasonable fit, and you already run Postgres. It avoids adding Kafka or routing notifications through another system.

| Option | Delivery characteristics | Operational fit |
|---|---|---|
| Postgres LISTEN/NOTIFY | Notifications are not persisted or replayable. Listeners can miss them while disconnected; payloads are limited. | Simplest if Postgres is already available. Keep payloads small—often just an ID—and have consumers fetch current state from the database. |
| Redis Pub/Sub | Also transient: subscribers disconnected at publish time miss messages. | A good alternative if Redis is already the natural notification path, but it does not provide stronger delivery guarantees than NOTIFY. |
| Kafka | Durable, replayable, and designed for high-throughput event streams. | More operational complexity than warranted if occasional loss is acceptable and replay is not required. |

A few caveats for LISTEN/NOTIFY:

  • Treat it as a wake-up signal, not the authoritative record of an event.
  • A listener should reconnect and, after reconnecting, check the database for work it may have missed.
  • If publishing must happen only when a database transaction commits, issue NOTIFY within that transaction.
  • Load-test your actual payload size and connection pattern; notifications are delivered to listening sessions, so a very large number of listeners can change the trade-off.

Choose Redis Pub/Sub instead if your application already publishes and consumes notifications through Redis and you want to avoid coupling this path to Postgres. Choose Kafka only if you later need durable retention, replay, consumer offsets, or stronger delivery guarantees.

Recommendation: use Redis Pub/Sub, assuming the service can tolerate losing messages while a subscriber is disconnected and doesn’t need replay or delivery guarantees.

At roughly 1,000 events/second, all three are capable in many ordinary deployments; the deciding factor is the delivery model and operational cost.

| Option | Delivery behavior | Fit for this service |
|---|---|---|
| Postgres LISTEN/NOTIFY | Notifications are transient: a disconnected listener misses them. Notifications issued inside a transaction are delivered only after commit. | Convenient if events are closely tied to database changes, but it couples notification traffic to the database. At this rate, monitor database load and keep payloads small. |
| Redis Pub/Sub | Transient: subscribers that are disconnected or unable to keep up miss messages. | A natural fit for best-effort notifications when Redis is already running. Simple and decoupled from database transactions. |
| Kafka | Persists events and supports replay, consumer offsets, and independent consumer groups. | Strong choice when you need durable delivery, replay, or multiple independent consumers, but likely more infrastructure and operational overhead than this requirement justifies. |

Use Redis Pub/Sub for the straightforward best-effort path. Keep the event payloads small, monitor subscriber disconnects and Redis health, and make consumers safe to handle duplicates if your application can produce them through its own retry logic.

Choose Postgres LISTEN/NOTIFY instead if notifications are specifically a signal that committed database state changed, and keeping that mechanism inside Postgres is valuable. Choose Kafka if “occasional loss is acceptable” stops being true, or you need replay, durable buffering, or multiple independently managed consumers.

Recommend Redis Pub/Sub for this service, assuming notifications are transient and clients can tolerate missed events. At 1,000 events/s, it is a straightforward fit, and you already run Redis. It delivers to currently connected subscribers with low latency, but does not store or replay messages; a disconnect can lose notifications.

| Option | Delivery behavior | Fit and tradeoff |
|---|---|---|
| Postgres LISTEN/NOTIFY | Notifications are transient and reach sessions listening when the notification is delivered. | No added system, but couples delivery to database connections and adds load to Postgres. Use it if notifications are already tightly tied to database transactions and volume is modest. |
| Redis Pub/Sub | Transient delivery to currently connected subscribers; no replay or acknowledgment. | Simple and low-latency for best-effort notifications. A Redis outage or subscriber disconnect can lose messages. |
| Kafka | Persists events and lets consumers resume from offsets. | Best when loss is unacceptable, replay is needed, or consumers need independent progress. Adds operational and implementation cost that occasional-loss requirements do not justify. |

If a missed event must eventually be recovered, none of these Pub/Sub-style choices is sufficient as configured: use a durable queue or log such as Kafka, or persist notifications in Postgres and let consumers track progress.

| Option | Delivery behavior | Operational fit |
|---|---|---|
| Postgres LISTEN/NOTIFY | Best-effort wakeups. Notifications are not stored for disconnected listeners; payloads are limited, and delivery depends on an active database session. | No new service, but uses Postgres connections and couples notifications to database activity. |
| Redis Pub/Sub | At-most-once: disconnected or slow subscribers can miss messages. No replay or acknowledgments. | Simple and low overhead with your existing Redis. |
| Kafka | Persists events and supports replay, consumer groups, and stronger delivery guarantees. | Most capable, but adds deployment and operational work that occasional loss does not justify. |

Use NOTIFY to signal that work is available, not as a durable event log. If listeners must recover after disconnects, write events to a table or use a durable queue; neither Postgres NOTIFY nor Redis Pub/Sub provides replay.

sample 0

Recommendation: use Redis Pub/Sub if notifications are transient and subscribers can tolerate missed events.

At roughly 1,000 events per second, all three options can handle the load with suitable provisioning. The key trade-off is delivery guarantees versus operational overhead:

| Option | Delivery behavior | Fit |
|---|---|---|
| Postgres LISTEN/NOTIFY | Listeners disconnected at publish time miss notifications. Notifications arrive after the transaction commits, and payloads are size-limited. | Suitable for modest, database-adjacent signaling, but adds work to Postgres and couples notifications to the database. |
| Redis Pub/Sub | Subscribers disconnected at publish time miss messages. Provides low-latency fan-out with little setup. | Best fit if Redis is already available and occasional message loss is acceptable. |
| Kafka | Durable, replayable log with consumer offsets and stronger recovery options. | More capability and operational overhead than needed when occasional loss is acceptable. |

Use Redis Pub/Sub for best-effort signals, such as “refresh this view,” not as the sole record of important business actions. Keep authoritative data in Postgres, and have consumers recover or refresh state after reconnecting.

If you later need replay, reliable processing, or per-consumer delivery tracking, consider Kafka or a Redis Streams-based design.

Recommendation: use Postgres LISTEN/NOTIFY if notifications can be transient and the service can recover by checking the source of truth. At about 1,000 events per second, it’s a reasonable fit—and you already run Postgres. It avoids adding Kafka or another notification system.

| Option | Delivery | Operational fit |
|---|---|---|
| Postgres LISTEN/NOTIFY | Transient, not persisted or replayable. Disconnected listeners can miss notifications; payloads are limited. | Simplest with Postgres already available. Send small payloads, often just an ID, and have consumers fetch current state from the database. |
| Redis Pub/Sub | Transient; subscribers miss messages published while they’re disconnected. | A good fit if Redis is already your natural notification path, but it offers no stronger delivery guarantees than NOTIFY. |
| Kafka | Durable and replayable; designed for high-throughput event streams. | Adds operational complexity if occasional loss is acceptable and replay isn’t needed. |

With LISTEN/NOTIFY:

  • Treat notifications as wake-up signals, not authoritative event records.
  • After reconnecting, check the database for work that may have been missed.
  • Issue NOTIFY within the transaction if it should be sent only when that transaction commits.
  • Load-test your payload sizes and connection pattern. A large number of listening sessions may change the trade-off.

Choose Redis Pub/Sub if notifications already flow through Redis and you want to avoid coupling this path to Postgres. Choose Kafka if you need durable retention, replay, consumer offsets, or stronger delivery guarantees.

s5b

Should we use a monorepo or separate repos? Team of 6 engineers maintaining a web app, a mobile app, and a shared API client library used by both. Give a recommendation.
baseline
sample 0

Use a monorepo, unless the web app, mobile app, and API client are owned or released very independently.

For a six-engineer team, the shared client creates meaningful coordination costs across repositories: changes often need matching updates, synchronized pull requests, and version bumps. In a monorepo, one change can update the client and both apps together, and shared tooling and review practices are easier to maintain.

A practical structure might be:

apps/
  web/
  mobile/
packages/
  api-client/

Keep clear package boundaries: each app should depend on the API client through an explicit package interface, with its own tests and build configuration. Use CI to run checks only for affected packages where possible, and make releases independently if the apps or client need different release schedules.

Choose separate repos instead if the client is a stable product consumed by other teams or external users, or if access controls, compliance, or substantially different release processes require separation. Otherwise, start with the monorepo; you can split a package out later if a real need emerges.

s1a

What's the difference between git merge and git rebase, and when should I use each?
baseline
sample 0

Git Merge vs. Git Rebase

Both commands integrate changes from one branch into another, but they do it differently and produce different histories.

Git Merge

Merge creates a new merge commit that ties the two branches' histories together.

git checkout main
git merge feature
      A---B---C  feature
     /         \
D---E---F---G---M  main

Characteristics:

  • Preserves the complete, true history, including when branches diverged and rejoined
  • Non-destructive: existing commits are never changed
  • Can clutter history with many merge commits
  • Safe on shared/public branches
Git Rebase

Rebase replays your commits on top of another branch, creating new commits with new hashes.

git checkout feature
git rebase main
Before:
      A---B---C  feature
     /
D---E---F---G  main

After:
              A'--B'--C'  feature
             /
D---E---F---G  main

Characteristics:

  • Produces a clean, linear history
  • Rewrites commit history (new SHAs)
  • Makes git log and git bisect easier to follow
  • Dangerous on shared branches if others have based work on the old commits
When to Use Each

Use merge when:

  • Integrating a finished feature into main (especially via a PR, where you want a record of the feature as a unit)
  • Working on a public/shared branch that others pull from
  • You want to preserve the exact history of what happened
  • You're unsure; it's the safer default

Use rebase when:

  • Updating your local feature branch with the latest changes from main
  • Cleaning up your own local commits before sharing (git rebase -i)
  • You want a linear history and your team prefers it
  • The commits haven't been pushed, or only you use the branch
The Golden Rule

> Never rebase commits that have been pushed to a shared branch that others may have based work on.

Rebasing rewrites history. If others have the old commits, you'll create diverging histories and painful conflicts. If you must force-push after rebasing your own branch, prefer git push --force-with-lease over --force.

Common Workflow Combining Both
# Keep your feature branch current (rebase)
git checkout feature
git rebase main

# Clean up messy commits (interactive rebase)
git rebase -i main

# Integrate into main (merge)
git checkout main
git merge feature
Handling Conflicts
  • Merge: resolve conflicts once, in a single merge commit.
  • Rebase: you may resolve conflicts commit by commit as each is replayed. Use git rebase --continue after fixing each, or git rebase --abort to bail out.
Quick Summary

| | Merge | Rebase |
|---|---|---|
| History | Preserves branching | Linear |
| Rewrites commits | No | Yes |
| Safe for shared branches | Yes | No |
| Conflict resolution | Once | Per commit |
| Best for | Integrating to main | Updating/cleaning local work |

If you'd like, I can look at your repo's current state and suggest which approach fits your situation.

485 words · 719 tok_est · out 1054 · 9.3s · 3.86 overall
cor 4 cla 5 amb 4 sca 3 rel 3 con 3 cmp 5
facts 5/5
sample 1

Git Merge vs. Git Rebase

Both integrate changes from one branch into another, but they do it differently.

Git Merge

Creates a new merge commit that ties the two branches' histories together.

Before:
      A---B---C  feature
     /
D---E---F---G    main

After `git checkout main && git merge feature`:
      A---B---C
     /         \
D---E---F---G---M  main

Pros

  • Non-destructive: existing commits are unchanged
  • Preserves the true history of when and how work was integrated
  • Safe for shared branches

Cons

  • History can become cluttered with merge commits
  • Non-linear history is harder to read with git log
Git Rebase

Replays your commits on top of another branch, creating new commits with new hashes.

After `git checkout feature && git rebase main`:
                  A'--B'--C'  feature
                 /
D---E---F---G                 main

You can then fast-forward main to include them, producing a perfectly linear history.

Pros

  • Clean, linear history
  • Easier to follow with git log, git bisect, and git blame
  • No extra merge commits

Cons

  • Rewrites history (new commit hashes)
  • Dangerous on shared branches
  • Conflicts may need to be resolved once per replayed commit
The Golden Rule

> Never rebase commits that have been pushed to a branch others are working on.

Rewriting shared history forces collaborators to reconcile diverging versions of the same commits, which gets messy fast.

When to Use Each

| Situation | Use |
|---|---|
| Updating your local feature branch with the latest main | Rebase |
| Cleaning up your own commits before opening a PR (rebase -i) | Rebase |
| Integrating a finished feature into main | Merge (or squash merge) |
| Working on a branch shared with others | Merge |
| You want to preserve the exact history of a long-lived branch | Merge |
| Resolving a conflict-heavy integration with lots of history | Merge (one resolution vs. many) |

Common Workflow
# On your feature branch: stay current with main
git fetch origin
git rebase origin/main

# Tidy up local commits before review
git rebase -i origin/main

# Push (force needed if you'd already pushed before rebasing)
git push --force-with-lease

# Then merge the PR into main via your platform (merge commit, squash, or rebase-and-merge)

Use --force-with-lease rather than --force. It refuses to overwrite the remote if someone else has pushed in the meantime.

Handy Tips
  • Abort if things go wrong: git merge --abort or git rebase --abort
  • Recover from a bad rebase: git reflog shows previous states you can git reset --hard to
  • Team conventions matter: many teams standardize on one approach (e.g., "squash merge only"), so check your project's norms
Short Version
  • Merge preserves history as it happened. It's safe and simple.
  • Rebase rewrites history to be clean and linear. It's powerful, but only use it on commits that are yours alone.

sample 2

Git Merge vs. Git Rebase

Both integrate changes from one branch into another, but they do it differently and produce different histories.

Git Merge

Creates a new merge commit that ties two branches together, preserving the history of both.

git checkout main
git merge feature
A---B---C---------M   main
     \           /
      D---E---F       feature

Pros

  • Non-destructive: existing commits are never changed
  • Accurately records when and how branches were integrated
  • Safe for shared/public branches

Cons

  • History can become cluttered with merge commits
  • Non-linear history is harder to read and bisect
Git Rebase

Replays your branch's commits on top of another branch, rewriting them as new commits.

git checkout feature
git rebase main
Before:
A---B---C   main
     \
      D---E---F   feature

After:
A---B---C   main
         \
          D'---E'---F'   feature

Pros

  • Clean, linear history
  • Easier to read with git log and to use with git bisect
  • No extra merge commits

Cons

  • Rewrites history (new commit hashes)
  • Dangerous on shared branches
  • Conflicts may need to be resolved once per replayed commit
When to Use Each

| Situation | Use |
|---|---|
| Updating your local feature branch with latest main | Rebase |
| Merging a finished feature into main | Merge (or squash merge) |
| Branch is shared with other developers | Merge |
| Cleaning up messy local commits before a PR | Interactive rebase (git rebase -i) |
| You want a permanent record of when a feature landed | Merge (--no-ff) |
| Commits are already pushed and others have pulled them | Merge |

The Golden Rule

> Never rebase commits that have been pushed to a shared branch that others are working from.

Rebasing rewrites history, so collaborators' copies diverge from yours, which leads to duplicate commits and painful conflicts.

A Common Workflow
  • Create a feature branch and commit locally.
  • Periodically git rebase main to stay current (while the branch is only yours).
  • Clean up with git rebase -i main.
  • Open a PR and merge into main using merge or squash merge.
Handy Tips
  • Abort if things go wrong: git rebase --abort or git merge --abort
  • Recover from a bad rebase: git reflog to find the old commit, then git reset --hard <hash>
  • If you've rebased a branch you already pushed (and it's only yours): use git push --force-with-lease rather than --force
  • Set pull to rebase by default: git config --global pull.rebase true
Summary
  • Merge preserves history as it happened. It's safe and simple.
  • Rebase rewrites history to make it clean and linear. Use it for local work only.

Many teams use both: rebase locally to keep branches tidy, merge to integrate into the main branch.

sample 0

Merge joins two branches by creating a new merge commit with two parents. History stays as it happened, and existing commits are untouched.

Rebase replays your branch's commits on top of another branch's tip. Each commit is rewritten with a new hash, so history looks linear, as if you'd started from the latest base.

Use merge when:

  • The branch is shared or already pushed and others may have based work on it.
  • You want an accurate record of when branches diverged and joined.
  • Merging a finished feature into main (often with --no-ff to keep the feature grouped).

Use rebase when:

  • Updating your local, unshared feature branch with the latest main (git rebase main), so the final diff is clean.
  • Tidying your own commits before review (git rebase -i: squash, reorder, reword).
  • You want linear history and your team enforces it.

Tradeoffs:

  • Merge: noisier history with merge commits, but conflicts are resolved once and nothing is rewritten.
  • Rebase: cleaner history, but conflicts may need resolving once per replayed commit, and it rewrites hashes.

Rule: never rebase commits that others have pulled. If you rebase a pushed branch, you must force-push (--force-with-lease), which breaks collaborators' clones.

Common workflow: rebase your private branch onto main while developing, then merge it into main via PR (merge commit or squash-merge, per team convention).

sample 1

Merge joins two branches by creating a new merge commit with two parents. History stays as it happened, with the branch structure visible. Existing commits are untouched.

Rebase replays your branch's commits on top of another branch's tip. Each commit is rewritten with a new hash, so history becomes linear and the branch looks like it was started from the latest base.

Use merge when:

  • The branch is shared or already pushed and others have based work on it.
  • You want to preserve the true history and branch context (e.g., merging a feature into main with --no-ff).
  • You want the safer option: nothing is rewritten, and conflicts are resolved once.

Use rebase when:

  • Updating your own local or unshared feature branch with the latest main (git rebase main), so the final history is linear.
  • Cleaning up commits before review (git rebase -i: squash, reorder, reword).
  • Your team prefers linear history (often paired with a fast-forward or squash merge into main).

Rules and risks:

  • Never rebase commits others have pulled. Rewritten hashes force them to reconcile diverged history, and you'll need git push --force-with-lease, which can destroy their work if misused.
  • Rebase can make you resolve the same conflict repeatedly, once per replayed commit. Merge resolves it once.
  • Rebase loses the original commit timestamps' context and the record of when integration happened. Merge commits keep it.

Common workflow: rebase your private feature branch onto main while developing, then merge it into main via PR (merge commit or squash). Use merge, not rebase, to bring changes into shared branches.

sample 2

Merge joins two branches by creating a new merge commit with two parents. History stays as it happened, and existing commits are untouched.

Rebase replays your branch's commits on top of another branch's tip, creating new commits (new SHAs). The result is linear history, as if you had started from the latest base.

Use merge when:

  • The branch is shared or already pushed and others may have based work on it.
  • You want an accurate record of when and how branches integrated.
  • Merging a finished feature into main (often with --no-ff to keep the feature grouped).

Use rebase when:

  • Updating your own local or private feature branch with the latest main (git rebase main), so the branch has no noise merge commits.
  • Cleaning up commits before review (git rebase -i: squash, reword, reorder, drop).
  • Your team wants linear history.

Tradeoffs:

  • Rebase rewrites history. After rebasing a pushed branch you need git push --force-with-lease, which breaks anyone else's checkout of it. Never rebase commits others have pulled.
  • Rebase resolves conflicts per replayed commit, so a long branch can mean repeated conflict fixes. Merge resolves them once.
  • Merge history gets cluttered with merge commits on busy branches, and git bisect and git log are harder to read.
  • Rebase loses the original context of when commits were made relative to the base.

Common workflow: rebase your private branch onto main while developing, then merge it into main (or squash-merge) via PR. Rebase for your own history, merge for shared history.

sample 0

Git Merge vs. Git Rebase

Both integrate one branch into another. Merge keeps true history by adding a merge commit; rebase replays your commits onto the target, producing a linear history with new SHAs.

Merge
git checkout main
git merge feature
      A---B---C  feature
     /         \
D---E---F---G---M  main
  • Creates a merge commit tying the histories together.
  • Preserves true history, including where branches diverged and rejoined.
  • Non-destructive: existing commits never change.
  • Safe on shared branches.
  • Many merge commits can clutter history.
Rebase
git checkout feature
git rebase main
Before:
      A---B---C  feature
     /
D---E---F---G  main

After:
              A'--B'--C'  feature
             /
D---E---F---G  main
  • Replays your commits on top of the target, creating new commits (new SHAs).
  • Gives a linear history, which makes git log and git bisect easier to follow.
  • Dangerous on shared branches if others have based work on the old commits.
When to use which

Merge when:

  • Integrating a finished feature into main, especially via PR, where you want the feature recorded as a unit.
  • The branch is shared and others pull from it.
  • You want the exact history preserved.
  • You're unsure. It's the safer default.

Rebase when:

  • Updating your local feature branch with latest main.
  • Cleaning up your own unpushed commits (git rebase -i).
  • Your team prefers linear history.
  • Only you use the branch, or the commits are unpushed.
Golden rule

Never rebase commits already pushed to a shared branch. Others holding the old commits will get diverging histories and painful conflicts. If you must force-push after rebasing your own branch, use git push --force-with-lease, not --force.

Combined workflow
# Keep feature current (rebase)
git checkout feature
git rebase main

# Clean up messy commits (interactive rebase)
git rebase -i main

# Integrate into main (merge)
git checkout main
git merge feature
Conflicts
  • Merge: resolve once, in the merge commit.
  • Rebase: resolve per replayed commit. Run git rebase --continue after each fix, or git rebase --abort to bail out.
Summary

| | Merge | Rebase |
|---|---|---|
| History | Preserves branching | Linear |
| Rewrites commits | No | Yes |
| Safe for shared branches | Yes | No |
| Conflict resolution | Once | Per commit |
| Best for | Integrating to main | Updating/cleaning local work |
