sample 1

Git Merge vs. Git Rebase

Merge preserves history as it happened and is safe on shared branches. Rebase rewrites your commits into a linear history and is safe only on commits that are yours alone.

Merge

Creates a merge commit that ties the two histories together.

Before:
      A---B---C  feature
     /
D---E---F---G    main

After `git checkout main && git merge feature`:
      A---B---C
     /         \
D---E---F---G---M  main

Pros

  • Non-destructive: existing commits are unchanged
  • Records when and how work was integrated
  • Safe for shared branches

Cons

  • Merge commits clutter history
  • Non-linear history is harder to read in git log
Rebase

Replays your commits on top of another branch as new commits with new hashes.

After `git checkout feature && git rebase main`:
                  A'--B'--C'  feature
                 /
D---E---F---G                 main

Fast-forwarding main to C' then gives a linear history.

Pros

  • Linear history, easier to follow with git log, git bisect, and git blame
  • No merge commits

Cons

  • Rewrites history (new hashes)
  • Dangerous on shared branches
  • Conflicts may need resolving once per replayed commit
Golden Rule

> Never rebase commits that have been pushed to a branch others are working on.

Rewriting shared history forces collaborators to reconcile diverging copies of the same commits.

When to Use Each

| Situation | Use |
|---|---|
| Updating your local feature branch with latest main | Rebase |
| Cleaning up your own commits before a PR (rebase -i) | Rebase |
| Integrating a finished feature into main | Merge (or squash merge) |
| Working on a branch shared with others | Merge |
| Preserving exact history of a long-lived branch | Merge |
| Conflict-heavy integration with lots of history | Merge (one resolution vs. many) |

Common Workflow
# On your feature branch: stay current with main
git fetch origin
git rebase origin/main

# Tidy up local commits before review
git rebase -i origin/main

# Push (force needed if you'd already pushed before rebasing)
git push --force-with-lease

# Then merge the PR into main via your platform (merge commit, squash, or rebase-and-merge)

Use --force-with-lease rather than --force: it refuses to overwrite the remote if someone else has pushed in the meantime.

Tips
  • Abort: git merge --abort or git rebase --abort
  • Recover from a bad rebase: git reflog shows earlier states; git reset --hard to one
  • Team conventions: many teams standardize on one approach (e.g., squash merge only). Check your project's norms.

Both integrate one branch into another. Merge keeps history as it happened; rebase rewrites it into a line.

Creates a merge commit joining the two branches and preserves both histories.

     \           /

  • Pros: existing commits never change; records when and how branches were integrated; safe on shared branches.

A---B---C   main

         \
          D'---E'---F'   feature
  • Pros: linear history, easier to read with git log and git bisect, no merge commits.

|---|---|

  • Run git rebase main periodically to stay current (only while the branch is yours alone).

s1b

In Python, what's the difference between `is` and `==`? When does it matter?
baseline
sample 0

is vs == in Python

== checks equality: do the two objects have the same value? It calls the object's __eq__ method.

is checks identity: are the two names pointing to the *exact same object* in memory? It's equivalent to id(a) == id(b).

Example
a = [1, 2, 3]
b = [1, 2, 3]
c = a

a == b   # True  (same contents)
a is b   # False (two separate list objects)
a is c   # True  (c is another name for the same object)
When it matters
1. Use is for singletons: None, True, False
if x is None:      # preferred
    ...

This is the idiomatic way because None is guaranteed to be a single instance, and is can't be fooled by a custom __eq__:

class Weird:
    def __eq__(self, other):
        return True

Weird() == None   # True (surprise!)
Weird() is None   # False
2. Use == for comparing values

Numbers, strings, lists, dicts, custom objects, and so on. Almost always, == is what you want.

3. Mutable objects and aliasing

is tells you whether modifying one will affect the other:

a = [1, 2]
b = a
b.append(3)
print(a)  # [1, 2, 3], because a is b
The gotcha: small-integer and string caching

CPython caches some objects as an optimization, so is can appear to work for values when it shouldn't be relied on:

a = 256
b = 256
a is b      # True (small ints -5..256 are cached)

a = 1000
b = 1000
a is b      # May be False (or True, depending on context!)
s1 = "hello"
s2 = "hello"
s1 is s2    # Often True due to string interning, but not guaranteed

These behaviors are implementation details, not language guarantees. Never use is to compare numbers or strings. (Python 3.8+ even emits a SyntaxWarning for x is 1 or x is "abc".)

Rule of thumb

| Situation | Use |
|---|---|
| Comparing to None | is / is not |
| Comparing to True/False (when you truly need identity) | is |
| Checking if two variables reference the same object | is |
| Comparing values (numbers, strings, collections, etc.) | == |

is vs == in Python

== checks equality: do the two objects have the same value? It calls the __eq__ method.

is checks identity: are the two names pointing to the exact same object in memory? It compares id(a) == id(b).

a = [1, 2, 3]
b = [1, 2, 3]
c = a

a == b   # True  (same contents)
a is b   # False (two separate list objects)
a is c   # True  (same object)
When it matters
1. Use is for singletons: None, True, False
if x is None:      # correct, idiomatic
if x == None:      # works usually, but can be fooled by custom __eq__

A class can override __eq__ to return anything, but nobody can override is. That makes is None reliable.

2. Use == for comparing values
if name == "alice":   # correct
if name is "alice":   # wrong, unreliable

Python 3.8+ emits a SyntaxWarning for is with literals.

3. Small-integer and string interning (the gotcha)

CPython caches some objects as an optimization, so is can seem to work by accident:

a = 256
b = 256
a is b      # True (small ints -5..256 are cached)

a = 1000
b = 1000
a is b      # may be False (implementation-dependent)

This is an implementation detail. Never rely on it.

4. Mutable objects and aliasing

is tells you whether mutating one will affect the other:

a = [1, 2]
b = a
b.append(3)
print(a)    # [1, 2, 3], because a and b are the same object
5. Custom __eq__
class AlwaysEqual:
    def __eq__(self, other):
        return True

x = AlwaysEqual()
x == None   # True (!)
x is None   # False
Rules of thumb
  • Use is / is not for None (and sometimes sentinel objects sentinel _MISSING = object()).
  • Use == / != for everything else.
  • Use is elsewhere only when you truly care about object identity.
Quick note on NaN
x = float('nan')
x == x   # False (NaN is not equal to itself)
x is x   # True

A case where the two operators give opposite answers on the same object.

374 words · 625 tok_est · out 812 · 8.2s · 4.14 overall
cor 5 cla 4 amb 4 sca 4 rel 4 con 3 cmp 5
facts 5/5
sample 2

is vs == in Python

== tests equality: do the two objects have the same value? It calls the __eq__ method.

is tests identity: are the two names pointing to the *exact same object* in memory? It's equivalent to id(a) == id(b).

a = [1, 2, 3]
b = [1, 2, 3]
c = a

a == b   # True  (same contents)
a is b   # False (two separate list objects)
a is c   # True  (same object)
When it matters
1. Comparing to None (use is)

None is a singleton, and == can be overridden by a class to return something unexpected.

if x is None:       # correct
if x is not None:   # correct
if x == None:       # works usually, but discouraged

The same applies to True and False singletons, though usually you'd just write if x:.

2. Comparing values (use ==)

For numbers, strings, lists, dicts, and most everything else, you want ==.

if name == "Alice":   # correct
if name is "Alice":   # wrong, unreliable (and a SyntaxWarning in 3.8+)
3. Small-int and string interning surprises

CPython caches some objects as an optimization, which makes is appear to work sometimes:

a = 256
b = 256
a is b     # True (small ints -5..256 are cached)

a = 1000
b = 1000
a is b     # may be False (implementation detail)

This is an implementation detail and not something to rely on. It's the main reason is for value comparison produces confusing bugs that seem to work in testing, then fail elsewhere.

4. Mutable objects and aliasing

is helps you check whether two names refer to the same mutable object, which matters when mutation through one name affects the other:

a = [1, 2]
b = a
b.append(3)
print(a)        # [1, 2, 3]
a is b          # True
5. Custom __eq__

A class can define what == means, but is cannot be overridden:

class AlwaysEqual:
    def __eq__(self, other):
        return True

x = AlwaysEqual()
x == None   # True (!)
x is None   # False
Rule of thumb
  • Use is / is not for None (and occasionally other singletons or sentinel objects).
  • Use == / != for everything else.
385 words · 636 tok_est · out 834 · 8.6s · 4.29 overall
cor 5 cla 5 amb 5 sca 4 rel 3 con 3 cmp 5
facts 5/5
anthropic-sonnet crisp
sample 0

== tests value equality (calls __eq__). is tests identity: whether both names point to the same object (same id()).

a = [1, 2]
b = [1, 2]
a == b   # True
a is b   # False, two separate lists
c = a
c is a   # True

When it matters:

  • Use is for singletons: None, True, False, and sentinels. x is None is correct because __eq__ can be overridden to return anything (NumPy arrays and pandas objects return arrays for == None), while is can't be overridden.
  • Use == for everything else. Comparing numbers, strings, containers, or custom objects with is is a bug even when it appears to work.

The trap: interning. CPython caches small ints (-5 to 256) and some strings, so is can give misleading results:

a = 256; b = 256
a is b   # True (cached)
a = 257; b = 257
a is b   # may be False (implementation-dependent, varies by context)

This is an implementation detail, not language semantics. Code relying on it will break unpredictably. Python 3.8+ emits a SyntaxWarning for is with a literal.

Mutability: is matters when you need to know if two references alias the same mutable object (mutating one affects the other). == only tells you the contents match right now.

Custom __eq__: if a class defines __eq__ without __hash__, instances become unhashable. And == falls back to identity if __eq__ isn't defined.

Rule: is for None/sentinels/explicit aliasing checks, == for all else.

sample 1

== tests value equality (calls __eq__). is tests identity: whether both names point to the same object (same id()).

a = [1, 2]
b = [1, 2]
a == b   # True
a is b   # False, two separate lists

When it matters

  • Use is for singletons: None, True, False, .... Write x is None, not x == None. A class can override __eq__ so that x == None returns something surprising (numpy arrays return an elementwise array), while is can't be overridden.
  • Use == for everything else. Comparing numbers, strings, containers, or custom objects with is gives results that depend on implementation details.

The trap: small-object caching

CPython caches small ints (-5 to 256) and interns some strings, so is can appear to work:

a = 256; b = 256
a is b   # True

a is b   # may be True or False (depends on context: same line/module vs. runtime-computed)

This is a CPython implementation detail, not language behavior. Code that relies on it breaks across versions and interpreters. Python 3.8+ also emits a SyntaxWarning for x is 1 or x is "abc".

Other legitimate is uses

  • Sentinel objects: _MISSING = object() then if arg is _MISSING.
  • Checking aliasing: whether two references share a mutable object.
  • Enum members: color is Color.RED works (they're singletons), though == also works.

Rule of thumb: is for None/sentinels/aliasing, == for values.

sample 2

== tests value equality (calls __eq__). is tests identity: whether both names point to the same object in memory (id(a) == id(b)).

a = [1, 2]
b = [1, 2]
a == b   # True
a is b   # False, two separate lists
c = a
c is a   # True

When it matters

  • Use is for singletons: None, True, False, and sentinels. x is None is correct because None is unique and is can't be overridden. x == None can return surprising results if a class defines a custom __eq__ (NumPy arrays return an elementwise array, for example).
  • Use == for everything else, including numbers, strings, and containers.

The trap: small-value caching

CPython caches small ints (-5 to 256) and interns some strings, so is can appear to work on values:

a = 256; b = 256
a is b   # True

a = 257; b = 257
a is b   # may be False (depends on context, e.g. REPL vs. same code block)

This is an implementation detail and varies by interpreter and version. Never use is to compare numbers or strings. Python 3.8+ emits a SyntaxWarning for is with a literal.

Custom __eq__

== follows whatever __eq__ defines. If you don't define it, the default falls back to identity, so == and is agree. is can't be customized.

Rule of thumb: is for None/sentinels/"same object" checks; == for "same value."

sample 0

is vs == in Python

  • == tests equality (same value). It calls __eq__.
  • is tests identity (same object in memory). It is equivalent to id(a) == id(b).
a = [1, 2, 3]
b = [1, 2, 3]
c = a

a == b   # True  (same contents)
a is b   # False (two separate list objects)
a is c   # True  (c is another name for the same object)
When to use which

Use is for singletons (None, True, False). None is a single instance, and is can't be fooled by a custom __eq__:

if x is None:
    ...

class Weird:
    def __eq__(self, other):
        return True

Weird() == None   # True (surprise!)
Weird() is None   # False

Use == to compare values (numbers, strings, lists, dicts, custom objects). This is almost always what you want.

Use is to check aliasing, i.e. whether mutating one name affects the other:

a = [1, 2]
b = a
b.append(3)
print(a)  # [1, 2, 3], because a is b
Gotcha: CPython caching

CPython caches some objects, so is can appear to work on numbers and strings. Those are implementation details, not language guarantees.

a = 256; b = 256
a is b      # True (ints -5..256 are cached)

a = 1000; b = 1000
a is b      # May be True or False, depending on context

s1 = "hello"; s2 = "hello"
s1 is s2    # Often True (interning), not guaranteed

Never use is to compare numbers or strings. Python 3.8+ emits a SyntaxWarning for x is 1 and x is "abc".

Rule of thumb

| Situation | Use |
|---|---|
| Comparing to None | is / is not |
| Comparing to True/False (when you need identity) | is |
| Checking whether two variables reference the same object | is |
| Comparing values (numbers, strings, collections, etc.) | == |

sample 1

is vs == in Python

  • == tests equality: same value. It calls __eq__.
  • is tests identity: same object. It compares id(a) == id(b).
a = [1, 2, 3]
b = [1, 2, 3]
c = a

a == b   # True  (same contents)
a is b   # False (two separate list objects)
a is c   # True  (same object)
Rules
  • Use is / is not for None, and for sentinels such as _MISSING = object().
  • Use == / != for everything else.
  • Use is elsewhere only when you need object identity, e.g. to check whether mutating one name affects another.
Why

None and singletons: use is. A class can override __eq__ to return anything, but is can't be overridden.

if x is None:   # correct, reliable
if x == None:   # can be fooled by a custom __eq__

class AlwaysEqual:
    def __eq__(self, other):
        return True

x = AlwaysEqual()
x == None   # True (!)
x is None   # False

Values: use ==. is with a literal is unreliable, and Python 3.8+ emits a SyntaxWarning for it.

if name == "alice":   # correct
if name is "alice":   # wrong

Don't rely on interning. CPython caches small ints (-5..256) and some strings, so is can work by accident:

a = 256; b = 256
a is b      # True (cached)

a = 1000; b = 1000
a is b      # may be False (implementation-dependent)

Aliasing. is shows whether two names share one mutable object:

a = [1, 2]
b = a
b.append(3)
print(a)    # [1, 2, 3], same object

NaN. The operators can disagree on the same object:

x = float('nan')
x == x   # False (NaN != itself)
x is x   # True

is vs == in Python

  • == tests equality (same value). It calls __eq__.
  • is tests identity (same object in memory). It's equivalent to id(a) == id(b).
a = [1, 2, 3]
b = [1, 2, 3]
c = a

a == b   # True  (same contents)
a is b   # False (two separate list objects)
a is c   # True  (same object)
Rules

Compare to None with is. None is a singleton, and a class can override == to return something unexpected. The same holds for True and False, though if x: is usually what you want.

if x is None:       # correct
if x is not None:   # correct
if x == None:       # usually works, discouraged

Compare values with ==. This covers numbers, strings, lists, dicts, and nearly everything else.

if name == "Alice":   # correct
if name is "Alice":   # wrong, unreliable; SyntaxWarning in 3.8+

Don't rely on CPython caching. CPython caches small ints (-5..256), so is can appear to work for values. It's an implementation detail. This is why is for value comparison passes in testing and then fails elsewhere.

a = 256; b = 256
a is b     # True (cached)

a = 1000; b = 1000
a is b     # may be False

Use is to check aliasing. When two names refer to the same mutable object, mutation through one is visible through the other.

a = [1, 2]
b = a
b.append(3)
print(a)        # [1, 2, 3]
a is b          # True

is can't be overridden; == can.

class AlwaysEqual:
    def __eq__(self, other):
        return True

x = AlwaysEqual()
x == None   # True (!)
x is None   # False
Rule of thumb
  • is / is not for None (and occasionally other singletons or sentinels).
  • == / != for everything else.

s2a

This Python prints ['a'] then ['a', 'b'], but I expected ['a'] then ['b']. Why, and how do I fix it? ```python def add_item(item, items=[]): items.append(item) return items print(add_item("a")) print(add_item("b")) ```
baseline
sample 0

This is Python's mutable default argument gotcha.

Why it happens

Default values are evaluated once, when the def statement runs, not each time the function is called. So items=[] creates a single list object that is stored on the function (add_item.__defaults__). Every call that doesn't pass items reuses that same list.

  • First call: the shared list is [], you append "a", and it becomes ['a'].
  • Second call: the shared list is still ['a'], you append "b", and it becomes ['a', 'b'].

You can see it directly:

print(add_item.__defaults__)  # (['a', 'b'],)

The fix: use None as a sentinel

def add_item(item, items=None):
    if items is None:
        items = []
    items.append(item)
    return items

print(add_item("a"))  # ['a']
print(add_item("b"))  # ['b']

Now a new list is created inside the function on each call, so nothing is shared.

Notes

  • Use is None rather than if not items:. Otherwise, if a caller passes in their own empty list, you'd silently replace it with a new one and they wouldn't see the append.
  • The same issue applies to any mutable default: lists, dicts, sets, or custom objects.
  • If you don't want to mutate the caller's list at all, you can build a new one instead:
def add_item(item, items=()):
    return [*items, item]

An immutable default like a tuple is safe here, since it can't be modified.

  • The shared-default behavior is occasionally used on purpose (e.g. simple caching or memoization), but it's usually clearer to do that explicitly with a module-level variable or functools.lru_cache.

s2b

I get `RuntimeError: dictionary changed size during iteration` from this. How do I fix it? ```python counts = {"a": 0, "b": 2, "c": 0} for k in counts: if counts[k] == 0: del counts[k] ```
baseline
sample 0

You can't add or remove keys from a dict while iterating over it. Iterate over a snapshot of the keys instead, or build a new dict.

Option 1: iterate over a copy of the keys

counts = {"a": 0, "b": 2, "c": 0}
for k in list(counts):
    if counts[k] == 0:
        del counts[k]

list(counts) creates a separate list of keys, so deleting from the dict is safe.

Option 2: rebuild the dict (usually the cleanest)

counts = {k: v for k, v in counts.items() if v != 0}

This creates a new dict, so any other references to the old counts won't see the change.

Option 3: filter in place without rebuilding

for k in [k for k, v in counts.items() if v == 0]:
    del counts[k]

This is the same idea as option 1, but collects the keys to delete first.

Which to use:

  • If nothing else holds a reference to the dict, use option 2.
  • If you need to mutate the original object (e.g., it's passed in from elsewhere), use option 1 or 3.

Changing the value of an existing key during iteration (counts[k] = 5) is fine. Only adding or removing keys triggers the error.

cor 5 cla 5 amb 5 sca 5 rel 4 con 4 cmp 5

You can't add or remove keys from a dict while iterating over it. The fix is to iterate over a snapshot of the keys, or build a new dict.

Option 3: collect keys first, then delete

to_delete = [k for k, v in counts.items() if v == 0]

    del counts[k]

Use Option 2 if nothing else holds a reference to the original dict. If other code references the same dict object and needs to see the change, use Option 1 or 3 so you mutate in place.

Result in all cases: {"b": 2}

Changing a value for an existing key during iteration is fine, since that doesn't change the dict's size. Only adding or removing keys triggers the error.

Or, if you don't need in-place mutation:

Use the first if other references to the same dict must see the change; use the comprehension otherwise (one pass, no del per key).

sample 0

Adding or removing dict keys while iterating raises RuntimeError. Iterate over a snapshot of the keys, or build a new dict.

Option 1: iterate over a copy of the keys

counts = {"a": 0, "b": 2, "c": 0}
for k in list(counts):
    if counts[k] == 0:
        del counts[k]

list(counts) is a separate list, so deleting from the dict is safe.

Option 2: rebuild the dict

counts = {k: v for k, v in counts.items() if v != 0}

This creates a new dict, so other references to the old counts won't see the change.

Option 3: collect keys, then delete

for k in [k for k, v in counts.items() if v == 0]:
    del counts[k]

Same as option 1, but the keys to delete are collected first.

Which to use:

  • Nothing else references the dict: option 2, usually the cleanest.
  • You must mutate the original object (e.g., it was passed in): option 1 or 3.

Changing the value of an existing key (counts[k] = 5) during iteration is fine. Only adding or removing keys raises the error.

sample 1

Adding or removing keys while iterating a dict raises RuntimeError. Iterate over a snapshot of the keys, or build a new dict.

Option 1: iterate over a copy of the keys (mutates in place)

for k in list(counts):
    if counts[k] == 0:
        del counts[k]

Option 2: build a new dict (usually cleanest)

counts = {k: v for k, v in counts.items() if v != 0}

Option 3: collect keys first, then delete (mutates in place)

to_delete = [k for k, v in counts.items() if v == 0]
for k in to_delete:
    del counts[k]

Use Option 2 unless other code holds a reference to the same dict and must see the change; then use Option 1 or 3. All three yield {"b": 2}.

Changing the value of an existing key during iteration is safe, since the dict's size doesn't change.

Write an implementation plan for adding per-user rate limiting to an existing Express API: 100 requests per minute per user, Redis-backed, and over-limit requests get a 429 with a Retry-After header. The API already has a JWT middleware that sets req.user.id. A coding agent will execute the plan.

Implementation Plan: Per-User Rate Limiting (Express + Redis)

Goal

Limit each authenticated user to 100 requests per minute. Over-limit requests receive HTTP 429 with a Retry-After header (seconds). State is stored in Redis so limits hold across multiple API instances.

Assumptions (verify in Step 1)
  • The existing JWT middleware sets req.user.id on authenticated routes.
  • Node.js and Express are in use; the package manager is npm/yarn/pnpm (use whichever the repo uses).
  • A Redis instance is available or can be provisioned (local via Docker for dev).
Step 1: Reconnaissance (no code changes)
  • Locate the Express app entry (app.js/server.js/src/index.ts) and note the middleware order.
  • Locate the JWT middleware and confirm where it is mounted (globally or per-router).
  • Check for an existing Redis client or config module. If one exists, reuse it.
  • Identify the language (JS vs TS), test framework (Jest/Mocha/Vitest), and the config/env convention (dotenv, config module).
  • Check for existing rate limiting (e.g., express-rate-limit) to avoid conflicts.
  • Identify any routes that should be exempt (health checks, login, webhooks) and unauthenticated routes.
Step 2: Algorithm Choice

Use a sliding window counter or a fixed window implemented atomically in Redis via a Lua script or MULTI. Recommended: fixed window with atomic INCR + EXPIRE for simplicity, unless the repo owners need smoother limiting.

  • Key: ratelimit:{userId}:{windowStart} where windowStart = floor(now / 60).
  • Logic (atomic, via Lua script):
  local current = redis.call('INCR', KEYS[1])
  if current == 1 then redis.call('EXPIRE', KEYS[1], ARGV[1]) end
  local ttl = redis.call('TTL', KEYS[1])
  return {current, ttl}
  • Using a Lua script avoids the race where a process crashes between INCR and EXPIRE, leaving a key with no TTL.
  • Known tradeoff: fixed windows permit up to 2x bursts at window boundaries. Note this in the code comments. If this is unacceptable, switch to a sliding-window-log using a sorted set (ZADD/ZREMRANGEBYSCORE/ZCARD) in a Lua script.
Step 3: Dependencies and Configuration
  • Install ioredis (or reuse the existing Redis client such as redis/node-redis).
  • Add environment variables, documented in .env.example:
  • REDIS_URL (e.g., redis://localhost:6379)
  • RATE_LIMIT_MAX=100
  • RATE_LIMIT_WINDOW_SECONDS=60
  • Centralize config in the existing config module with defaults of 100 and 60.
Step 4: Redis Client Module

Create src/lib/redis.(js|ts) (skip if one exists):

  • Export a singleton client built from REDIS_URL.
  • Attach an error event handler that logs (prevents unhandled error crashes).
  • Configure sensible maxRetriesPerRequest and connectTimeout so requests do not hang when Redis is down.
Step 5: Rate Limit Middleware

Create src/middleware/rateLimiter.(js|ts):

  • Export a factory: createRateLimiter({ max, windowSeconds, redis }) returning (req, res, next). This makes it testable with injected dependencies.
  • Behavior:
  • If req.user?.id is missing, do not apply per-user limiting. Call next() (the auth middleware is responsible for rejecting unauthenticated requests). Log at debug level. Do not fall back to IP unless the owners request it.
  • Run the Lua script (load once with defineCommand in ioredis, or use EVALSHA with fallback) with key ratelimit:${userId}:${windowStart}.
  • Set response headers on every response:
  • X-RateLimit-Limit: 100
  • X-RateLimit-Remaining: max(0, max - current)
  • X-RateLimit-Reset: <unix timestamp of window end>
  • If current > max:
  • Set Retry-After: <ttl seconds> (integer, minimum 1).
  • Respond 429 with JSON: { "error": "Too Many Requests", "message": "Rate limit exceeded. Try again in N seconds.", "retryAfter": N }.
  • Do not call next().
  • Otherwise call next().
  • Failure mode (fail open): wrap Redis calls in try/catch. On Redis error, log the error with context, then call next() so a Redis outage does not take down the API. Make this behavior configurable via RATE_LIMIT_FAIL_OPEN (default true). If false, respond 503.
  • Use the user ID only as a Redis key component (do not log tokens or PII beyond the ID).
Step 6: Wire Into the App
  • Mount the rate limiter after the JWT middleware, so req.user.id is populated, and before route handlers.
  • If JWT is applied per-router, mount the limiter in the same place, immediately after the auth middleware.
  • Exclude exempt routes (health checks, etc.) by mounting the limiter only on the relevant routers or by ordering routes so exempt ones are registered first.
  • If the app runs behind a proxy, this plan does not require trust proxy because limiting is by user, not IP. Make no change there.
Step 7: Tests

Using the repo's test framework, plus supertest:

Unit tests (middleware with a mock or in-memory Redis such as ioredis-mock; note ioredis-mock Lua support may be limited, so prefer a real Redis container for integration tests):

  • Requests 1–100 pass with 200 and correct X-RateLimit-Remaining values.
  • Request 101 returns 429 with Retry-After an integer between 1 and 60.
  • Two different users have independent counters.
  • After the window expires (use fake timers or a short windowSeconds such as 1–2s), requests succeed again.
  • Missing req.user bypasses limiting and calls next().
  • Redis failure with fail-open calls next(). With fail-closed it returns 503.
  • Concurrency: fire 150 parallel requests for one user and assert exactly 100 succeed and 50 get 429.

Integration test: Boot the app with the real JWT middleware and a test Redis, issue requests with a valid token, and verify the full flow including headers.

Step 8: Local Dev and CI
  • Add a Redis service to docker-compose.yml if one exists (redis:7-alpine).
  • Add a Redis service to the CI config for integration tests.
  • Update the README with: the limit, env vars, response format, and how to run Redis locally.
Step 9: Verification Checklist
  • [ ] npm test passes, including new tests.
  • [ ] Lint and type checks pass.
  • [ ] Manual test: loop 101 authenticated curl requests; the 101st returns 429 with Retry-After.
  • [ ] redis-cli TTL ratelimit:<id>:<window> shows a TTL on every key (no keys without expiry).
  • [ ] Stopping Redis does not crash the API (fail-open behavior confirmed).
  • [ ] Two app instances share the same counter.
Out of Scope (do not implement unless asked)
  • IP-based limiting for unauthenticated routes.
  • Per-endpoint or per-tier limits.
  • Admin override or allowlist tooling.
Deliverables
  • src/lib/redis (if not existing)
  • src/middleware/rateLimiter
  • Updated app wiring, config, .env.example, docker-compose/CI, README
  • Tests as described above
Notes for the Executing Agent
  • Match the repo's existing code style, module system (CJS/ESM), and directory layout. Adjust paths above accordingly.
  • Make small, reviewable commits: (1) config + Redis client, (2) middleware, (3) wiring, (4) tests, (5) docs/infra.
  • If any assumption in Step 1 turns out false (for example req.user.id is named differently), stop and adapt the plan rather than guessing, and report the discrepancy in the PR description.

Implementation Plan: Per-User Rate Limiting (Express + Redis)

Goal

Limit each authenticated user to 100 requests per 60 seconds. Over-limit requests receive 429 Too Many Requests with a Retry-After header (seconds). State lives in Redis so limits hold across multiple API instances.

Assumptions (verify in step 1)
  • JWT middleware runs before the limiter and sets req.user.id.
  • Node/Express project with a package manager already in use (npm/yarn/pnpm).
  • A Redis instance is available, with its URL provided via environment variable.
Step 1: Reconnaissance (no changes yet)
  • Locate the app entry point (where app.use(...) calls are registered) and the JWT middleware. Note the order of middleware and which routes are protected.
  • Check for an existing Redis client (grep -r "redis" src package.json). Reuse it if present; don't create a second connection.
  • Identify the test framework, config/env-loading pattern, logger, and error-handling conventions. Follow them.
  • Check whether the app sits behind a proxy/load balancer (affects only unauthenticated fallback, see Step 4).
Step 2: Dependencies
  • If no Redis client exists, install redis (node-redis v4+) or ioredis, whichever matches the codebase's existing style. Default to ioredis if there's no precedent.
  • Do not add express-rate-limit or similar; a small custom limiter gives exact control over the atomic Lua logic and headers.
  • Dev dependency for tests if needed: ioredis-mock or use a real Redis in the test environment (prefer real Redis if CI already provides it, since Lua scripting is poorly emulated by mocks).
Step 3: Configuration

Add to the existing config module / .env.example:

REDIS_URL=redis://localhost:6379
RATE_LIMIT_MAX=100
RATE_LIMIT_WINDOW_SECONDS=60

Read these with defaults of 100 and 60. Parse as integers and fail fast on invalid values.

Step 4: Implement the Middleware

Create src/middleware/rateLimit.js (match the project's language and directory conventions, e.g. .ts).

Algorithm: fixed window counter with atomic Lua script (simple, cheap, and atomic). Note the known tradeoff: up to 2x burst at window boundaries. If that is unacceptable, switch to a sliding-window-log using a sorted set; mention this in the PR description but implement the fixed window by default.

Key: ratelimit:{userId}:{windowStartEpochSeconds}, where windowStart = floor(now / window) * window. This ties each key to a window, so Retry-After is simply windowStart + window - now.

Atomic operation (single Lua script via EVAL, avoiding an INCR/EXPIRE race where a crash between them leaves a key with no TTL):

local current = redis.call('INCR', KEYS[1])
if current == 1 then
  redis.call('EXPIRE', KEYS[1], ARGV[1])
end
return current

Pass ARGV[1] = windowSeconds (optionally plus a small buffer, e.g. +1s). Load the script once (defineCommand in ioredis, or SCRIPT LOAD/EVALSHA in node-redis).

Middleware behavior:

  • Read req.user?.id. If missing, the limiter was mounted before auth or the route is public. Don't silently skip: either (a) fall back to limiting by req.ip for unauthenticated traffic, or (b) call next() if the route is intentionally public. Choose (a) only if the app has trust proxy configured correctly; otherwise log a warning and skip. Document the choice in a code comment.
  • Compute now, windowStart, and the key; run the script to get count.
  • Set headers on every response:
  • RateLimit-Limit: 100
  • RateLimit-Remaining: max(0, 100 - count)
  • RateLimit-Reset: <seconds until window end>
  • If count > limit:
  • Set Retry-After: <seconds until window end> (integer, minimum 1).
  • Respond 429 with JSON in the project's existing error format, e.g. { "error": "Too Many Requests", "retryAfter": N }.
  • Do not call next().
  • Otherwise call next().

Failure mode (Redis down/error): fail open. Catch the error, log it at error level with the user id, and call next() so a Redis outage doesn't take down the API. Add a short connect/command timeout (e.g. 200–500 ms) so a hung Redis doesn't add latency. Make this behavior a single clearly-named constant or config flag (RATE_LIMIT_FAIL_OPEN=true) so it can be flipped.

Export a factory, createRateLimiter({ redis, limit, windowSeconds }), for testability, and instantiate it with config values in the app wiring.

Step 5: Wire It In
  • Register the limiter after the JWT middleware and before route handlers: app.use(jwtMiddleware); app.use(rateLimiter); (or on the specific authenticated router if the app has public routes).
  • Exempt health checks (/health, /ready) by mounting the limiter below them or via a path skip.
  • Ensure the Redis client is closed in the existing graceful-shutdown handler, if one exists.
Step 6: Tests

Cover with the project's test framework (supertest or similar):

  • Requests 1–100 within a window return success and have decreasing RateLimit-Remaining.
  • Request 101 returns 429, with a Retry-After header that is an integer between 1 and 60, and the correct error body.
  • Two different users have independent counters.
  • Counter resets in a new window (use fake timers or inject a now() function into the factory, which is why the factory should accept an optional clock).
  • Redis failure results in the request passing through (fail open) and an error being logged.
  • Key has a TTL set (TTL key > 0) after the first request.
  • Concurrency: fire ~150 parallel requests for one user and assert exactly 100 succeed.
Step 7: Documentation
  • Add the new env vars to .env.example and the README (or API docs).
  • Document the 429 response shape and headers for API consumers.
Step 8: Verification Checklist
  • [ ] Existing test suite passes.
  • [ ] New tests pass.
  • [ ] Manual check: for i in $(seq 1 105); do curl -s -o /dev/null -w "%{http_code}\n" -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" localhost:3000/api/...; done shows 100 successes followed by 429s.
  • [ ] redis-cli --scan --pattern 'ratelimit:*' shows keys, and TTL is ≤ 60.
  • [ ] Lint/type-check passes.
  • [ ] No new Redis connection created if one already existed.
Out of Scope (mention in PR, don't build)
  • Per-route or per-tier limits.
  • Sliding-window precision.
  • IP-based limiting beyond the fallback in Step 4.
  • Metrics/dashboards for rate-limit hits (consider logging a structured line on each 429 as a cheap start).
Deliverable

A single PR containing the middleware, wiring, config changes, tests, and docs, with a description noting the fixed-window tradeoff and the fail-open decision.

Implementation Plan: Per-User Rate Limiting (Express + Redis)

Goal

Limit each authenticated user to 100 requests per 60 seconds, backed by Redis. Over-limit requests receive HTTP 429 with a Retry-After header (seconds). The existing JWT middleware sets req.user.id.

Design Decisions
  • Algorithm: Fixed-window counter via an atomic Lua script (INCR + EXPIRE on first hit, returns count and TTL). It's simple, cheap (one Redis round trip), and race-free. Note the known tradeoff: a user can burst up to 2x at window boundaries. If that's unacceptable, switch to a sliding-window log or token bucket (see "Optional upgrades").
  • Key: ratelimit:{userId}:{windowSeconds}. Expiry is set to the window length on first increment.
  • Placement: Mounted after the JWT middleware so req.user.id exists. Unauthenticated requests are skipped by this middleware (they should already be rejected by auth, or be handled by a separate IP-based limiter, which is out of scope).
  • Failure mode: Fail open if Redis is unavailable (log the error, allow the request), so a Redis outage doesn't take down the API. Make this configurable.
  • Library: Use the existing Redis client if the project has one; otherwise add ioredis. Don't add a rate-limit library; the custom logic is small and gives exact control over headers.
Steps
1. Reconnaissance (no changes yet)
  • Locate the Express app setup file (e.g., app.js/server.ts) and note where the JWT middleware is mounted.
  • Check package.json for an existing Redis client (ioredis, redis) and any existing Redis connection module/config. Reuse it if present.
  • Identify the language (JS vs TS), module system (CJS vs ESM), test framework, and lint/format conventions. Match them.
  • Identify env/config conventions (dotenv, config module) and how REDIS_URL or equivalent is provided.
2. Dependencies
  • If no Redis client exists: npm install ioredis (and @types/ioredis is not needed; ioredis ships types).
  • For testing, check whether ioredis-mock is suitable. Prefer a real Redis in integration tests if the repo already uses one, because Lua scripts are poorly supported in some mocks.
3. Configuration

Add the following settings, following the project's existing config pattern, with these defaults:

| Variable | Default | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| RATE_LIMIT_MAX | 100 | Requests allowed per window |
| RATE_LIMIT_WINDOW_SECONDS | 60 | Window length |
| RATE_LIMIT_FAIL_OPEN | true | Allow requests if Redis errors |
| REDIS_URL | (existing) | Redis connection |

4. Implement the middleware

Create src/middleware/rateLimit.(js|ts) (match the repo's layout). Export a factory createRateLimiter({ redis, max, windowSeconds, failOpen }) returning an Express middleware.

Lua script (atomic):

local current = redis.call('INCR', KEYS[1])
if current == 1 then
  redis.call('EXPIRE', KEYS[1], ARGV[1])
end
local ttl = redis.call('TTL', KEYS[1])
if ttl < 0 then
  redis.call('EXPIRE', KEYS[1], ARGV[1])
  ttl = tonumber(ARGV[1])
end
return {current, ttl}

The ttl < 0 branch repairs a key that somehow lost its expiry, which would otherwise lock a user out permanently.

Middleware behavior:

  • If !req.user?.id, call next() and return (no limiting without an identity).
  • Run the script (use redis.defineCommand in ioredis, or eval/evalsha) with key ratelimit:${req.user.id}:${windowSeconds}.
  • Set headers on every response:
  • RateLimit-Limit: <max>
  • RateLimit-Remaining: <max(0, max - current)>
  • RateLimit-Reset: <ttl seconds>
  • If current > max:
  • Set Retry-After: <ttl> (integer seconds, minimum 1).
  • Respond 429 with JSON: { "error": "Too Many Requests", "retryAfter": <ttl> }. Match the API's existing error response shape if one exists.
  • Otherwise call next().
  • On Redis error: log it using the project's logger; if failOpen, call next(), else respond 503.
5. Wire it in
  • In the app setup, mount the limiter immediately after the JWT middleware and before the route handlers:
  app.use(jwtMiddleware);
  app.use(createRateLimiter({ redis, max, windowSeconds, failOpen }));
  • If JWT middleware is applied per-router rather than globally, mount the limiter on those same routers right after it.
  • Exempt health-check/metrics endpoints (e.g., /health) by mounting them before the limiter or adding a skip list.
  • If the app runs behind a proxy, no change is needed since the limit is per user, not per IP.
6. Tests

Unit/integration tests using the project's framework (and supertest if already used):

  • Requests 1–100 within a window return success; request 101 returns 429.
  • The 429 response includes Retry-After as an integer between 1 and 60.
  • RateLimit-Remaining decrements correctly.
  • Two different users have independent counters.
  • After the window expires (use a short window like 1–2 seconds in test config, or manipulate the TTL), the user can make requests again.
  • Requests without req.user bypass the limiter.
  • Redis failure with failOpen=true allows the request and logs an error; with failOpen=false returns 503.
  • Concurrency: fire ~150 parallel requests for one user and assert exactly 100 succeed.
7. Documentation
  • Add a short section to the README/API docs: limit, window, headers returned, 429 response format, and the new env variables.
  • Add the new env vars to .env.example.
8. Verification checklist
  • [ ] Existing test suite passes.
  • [ ] New tests pass.
  • [ ] Lint/type-check passes.
  • [ ] Manual check: script 101 requests with curl using a valid JWT; confirm the 101st returns 429 with Retry-After.
  • [ ] redis-cli TTL ratelimit:<id>:60 shows a positive TTL, and keys expire on their own.
Optional upgrades (do not implement unless asked)
  • Sliding-window log or token bucket to remove boundary bursts.
  • Per-route or per-tier limits (e.g., higher limits for paid users).
  • A separate IP-based limiter for unauthenticated endpoints such as login.
  • Redis Cluster: key names are single-key so the script is cluster-safe, but verify hash-slot behavior if clustering is used.
Constraints for the executing agent
  • Don't modify the JWT middleware.
  • Don't change existing route behavior beyond adding the limiter and headers.
  • Keep changes minimal and follow existing code style.
  • If any assumption above conflicts with what you find in the repo (e.g., a different auth flow or no Redis config), stop and report the conflict instead of guessing.

  • REDIS_URL (required)

3. Redis client module

Create src/lib/redis.js (match repo's module style, ESM vs CJS) exporting a single shared client:

  • new Redis(process.env.REDIS_URL, { maxRetriesPerRequest: 1, enableOfflineQueue: false, commandTimeout: 100 })
  • Attach an error listener that logs; without it ioredis throws on unhandled error events.

If a shared client already exists, import it instead.

4. Middleware

Create src/middleware/rateLimit.js.

Lua script (via redis.defineCommand or eval):

local c = redis.call('INCR', KEYS[1])
if c == 1 then redis.call('EXPIRE', KEYS[1], ARGV[1]) end
local ttl = redis.call('TTL', KEYS[1])
if ttl < 0 then redis.call('EXPIRE', KEYS[1], ARGV[1]); ttl = tonumber(ARGV[1]) end
return {c, ttl}

Middleware logic:

  • If !req.user?.id, call next().
  • Key: ratelimit:${req.user.id} (one key per user, TTL provides the window; no bucket math needed).
  • Run the script; get [count, ttl].
  • Always set headers: RateLimit-Limit: 100, RateLimit-Remaining: max(0, 100 - count), RateLimit-Reset: ttl.
  • If count > max: set Retry-After: ttl (integer seconds, minimum 1) and respond 429 with JSON { "error": "Too Many Requests", "retryAfter": ttl }. Use the repo's existing error format if it has one.
  • Otherwise next().
  • Wrap in try/catch: on error, log and next() (fail open).

Export a factory createRateLimiter({ max, windowSec, redis }) so tests can inject a client and small limits.

5. Mount
  • Register app.use(rateLimiter) immediately after the JWT middleware. If JWT is applied per-router rather than globally, mount the limiter on the same routers directly after it.
  • Exempt health checks (/health, /healthz, or whatever exists) by mounting them before the limiter.
6. Tests

Use the repo's test framework. Prefer a real Redis (docker/CI service) or ioredis-mock if the repo already avoids real services. Cover:

  • Request 100 returns 200; request 101 returns 429.
  • 429 response has Retry-After as an integer between 1 and 60.
  • Two different user IDs have independent counters.
  • After the window expires (use a 1s window via the factory), the user is allowed again.
  • Redis failure (inject a client whose script call rejects) results in the request passing through.
  • Request with no req.user is not limited and doesn't touch Redis.
7. Verify
  • Run the full test suite and linter; all must pass.
  • Manual check if a dev server is feasible: loop 101 authenticated curl requests and confirm the last returns 429 with Retry-After.
Out of scope

Per-route limits, IP-based limiting, sliding-window accuracy, admin overrides, metrics. Mention in the summary if any seem needed, but don't build them.

Deliverable

Code and tests committed on a branch, plus a short summary listing files changed, new env vars, and any assumption that turned out wrong.

Plan: Per-user rate limiting (Express + Redis)

Decisions
  • Algorithm: sliding window counter via a Lua script (atomic, no race between check and increment). Fixed window allows 2x bursts at boundaries; sliding window log costs O(n) memory per user. Sliding window counter (two adjacent fixed buckets, weighted) is the compromise.
  • Client: ioredis. If the repo already uses redis (node-redis), use that instead and adapt the script call.
  • Placement: after JWT middleware, before route handlers. Unauthenticated requests (no req.user) skip this limiter; IP-based limiting is out of scope.
  • Failure mode: fail open. If Redis errors or times out (>100 ms), log and allow the request. Change to fail closed only if the user says so.
Steps
  • Inspect the repo first. Find: where JWT middleware is mounted in the app setup, whether a Redis client already exists, config/env conventions, test framework, and TypeScript vs JS. Match existing conventions. Do not add a second Redis client if one exists.
  • Dependencies. Add ioredis if no Redis client is present. For tests, use ioredis-mock only if it supports defineCommand/eval; otherwise run tests against a real Redis (docker or REDIS_URL from env).
  • Config. Add env vars with defaults:
  • REDIS_URL (required, no default)
  • RATE_LIMIT_MAX=100
  • RATE_LIMIT_WINDOW_SEC=60
  • Create middleware/rateLimit.(js|ts) exporting createRateLimiter({ redis, max, windowSec }) that returns Express middleware.

Behavior per request:

  • If !req.user?.id, call next().
  • Key prefix: rl:{userId}:. Current bucket = floor(now / windowSec); keys are rl:{userId}:{bucket} and rl:{userId}:{bucket-1}.
  • Lua script (single EVAL, keys = current and previous bucket):
  • Read previous count p and current count c.
  • Elapsed fraction f = (now mod window) / window.
  • Estimated e = p * (1 - f) + c.
  • If e >= max: return {0, retryAfterSec} without incrementing.
  • Else INCR current, EXPIRE current to 2 * windowSec, return {1, remaining}.
  • Pass now from Node as an argument (ms). Redis server time via TIME is preferable if multiple app servers have clock skew; use redis.call('TIME') inside the script if so (Redis ≥5 with script replication of effects, which is the default in 5+/7).
  • Compute retryAfterSec as the time until the estimate drops below max, minimum 1, integer, rounded up. A simple acceptable approximation: seconds until the current bucket ends (ceil(windowSec - now mod windowSec)), min 1.
  • Always set headers: RateLimit-Limit, RateLimit-Remaining (floor, min 0).
  • On block: set Retry-After: <integer seconds>, respond 429 with JSON { "error": "rate_limit_exceeded", "retryAfter": <n> }. Match the API's existing error body shape if it has one.
  • Wrap Redis call in try/catch with a 100 ms timeout; on error, log with the existing logger and next().
  • Wire it up. In app setup, mount app.use(rateLimiter) immediately after the JWT middleware. If JWT is applied per-router rather than globally, mount the limiter on the same routers. Exclude health-check routes (e.g. /health) if they sit behind JWT.
  • Tests. Cover:
  • 100 requests in a window succeed; the 101st returns 429 with an integer Retry-After ≥ 1.
  • Two different users have independent counters.
  • Request without req.user is not limited.
  • Counter recovers after the window passes (use a short windowSec such as 1–2 in the test, or fake time by injecting now).
  • Redis failure (point client at a closed port) results in the request passing through.
  • Concurrency: fire 150 parallel requests for one user, assert exactly 100 succeed.
  • Verify. Run the full test suite and linter. Manually start the server with a local Redis and hit an authenticated endpoint 101 times (for loop with curl), confirming the 429 and the header.
Deliverables
  • Middleware file, config changes, wiring change, tests.
  • A short note in the README or .env.example for the three env vars.
Out of scope

IP-based limiting, per-route limits, tiered limits per plan, Redis Cluster key-slot handling (both keys share the {userId} prefix; if the deployment is a cluster, use the hash tag rl:{userId}:... with braces so both keys land on one slot, as written above).

Report back

List any deviations from this plan, especially if an existing Redis client or JWT mounting pattern forced a different structure.

Plan: Per-User Rate Limiting (Express + Redis)

Goal: Limit each authenticated user to 100 requests/minute. Over-limit requests get HTTP 429 with Retry-After (seconds). Counters live in Redis so limits hold across API instances.

Assumptions (verify in Step 1; if any is false, stop, adapt, and report it in the PR description):

  • The JWT middleware sets req.user.id.
  • Node/Express; package manager is whichever the repo uses.
  • Redis is available (Docker locally for dev).

Match the repo's style, module system (CJS/ESM), and layout; adjust paths below. Commit in this order: (1) config + Redis client, (2) middleware, (3) wiring, (4) tests, (5) docs/infra.

Step 1: Recon (no code changes)
  • Find the app entry (app.js/server.js/src/index.ts) and note middleware order.
  • Find the JWT middleware and whether it is mounted globally or per-router.
  • Look for an existing Redis client/config; reuse it if present.
  • Identify JS vs TS, test framework (Jest/Mocha/Vitest), and config/env convention.
  • Check for existing rate limiting (e.g., express-rate-limit) to avoid conflicts.
  • List routes to exempt (health checks, login, webhooks) and unauthenticated routes.
Step 2: Algorithm

Fixed window, atomic in Redis via Lua. Key: ratelimit:{userId}:{windowStart}, windowStart = floor(now / 60).

local current = redis.call('INCR', KEYS[1])
if current == 1 then redis.call('EXPIRE', KEYS[1], ARGV[1]) end
local ttl = redis.call('TTL', KEYS[1])
return {current, ttl}

Lua avoids a crash between INCR and EXPIRE leaving a key with no TTL.

Tradeoff: fixed windows allow up to 2x bursts at window boundaries. Note this in a code comment. If that is unacceptable, switch to a sliding-window log (sorted set with ZADD/ZREMRANGEBYSCORE/ZCARD in Lua).

Step 3: Dependencies and config
  • Install ioredis (or reuse the existing client).
  • Add to .env.example and the existing config module:
  • REDIS_URL (e.g., redis://localhost:6379)
  • RATE_LIMIT_MAX (default 100)
  • RATE_LIMIT_WINDOW_SECONDS (default 60)
  • RATE_LIMIT_FAIL_OPEN (default true)
Step 4: Redis client

Create src/lib/redis.(js|ts) unless one exists:

  • Export a singleton built from REDIS_URL.
  • Attach an error handler that logs, so errors don't crash the process.
  • Set maxRetriesPerRequest and connectTimeout so requests don't hang when Redis is down.
Step 5: Middleware

Create src/middleware/rateLimiter.(js|ts) exporting createRateLimiter({ max, windowSeconds, redis }), which returns (req, res, next). Injected dependencies keep it testable.

Behavior:

  • No req.user?.id: call next() and log at debug. Auth middleware rejects unauthenticated requests. No IP fallback unless owners ask.
  • Counting: run the Lua script (load once via defineCommand in ioredis, or EVALSHA with fallback) with key ratelimit:${userId}:${windowStart}.
  • Headers on every response:
  • X-RateLimit-Limit: <max>
  • X-RateLimit-Remaining: max(0, max - current)
  • X-RateLimit-Reset: <unix timestamp of window end>
  • If current > max: set Retry-After: <ttl> (integer, min 1) and respond 429 with { "error": "Too Many Requests", "message": "Rate limit exceeded. Try again in N seconds.", "retryAfter": N }. Don't call next().
  • Otherwise: call next().
  • Redis error: wrap Redis calls in try/catch and log with context. If RATE_LIMIT_FAIL_OPEN is true, call next() so an outage doesn't take down the API. If false, respond 503.
  • Logging: use the user ID only as a key component. Don't log tokens or PII beyond the ID.
Step 6: Wiring
  • Mount the limiter immediately after the JWT middleware and before route handlers. If JWT is per-router, mount it there.
  • Exempt routes (health checks, etc.): mount the limiter only on the relevant routers, or register exempt routes first.
  • Don't change trust proxy; limiting is by user, not IP.
Step 7: Tests

Use the repo's framework plus supertest. For unit tests use a mock Redis such as ioredis-mock, but its Lua support may be limited, so prefer a real Redis container for anything exercising the script.

Cases:

  • Requests 1–100 return 200 with correct X-RateLimit-Remaining.
  • Request 101 returns 429 with integer Retry-After in 1–60.
  • Two users have independent counters.
  • After the window expires (fake timers or windowSeconds of 1–2), requests succeed again.
  • Missing req.user bypasses limiting and calls next().
  • Redis failure: fail-open calls next(); fail-closed returns 503.
  • Concurrency: 150 parallel requests for one user yield exactly 100 successes and 50 429s.

Integration test: boot the app with the real JWT middleware and a test Redis, send requests with a valid token, and verify the full flow including headers.

Step 8: Dev, CI, docs
  • Add redis:7-alpine to docker-compose.yml if one exists.
  • Add a Redis service to CI for integration tests.
  • Update the README: limit, env vars, response format, running Redis locally.
Step 9: Verification
  • [ ] Tests pass (including new ones); lint and type checks pass.
  • [ ] Manual: 101 authenticated curl requests in a loop; the 101st returns 429 with Retry-After.
  • [ ] redis-cli TTL ratelimit:<id>:<window> shows a TTL on every key.
  • [ ] Stopping Redis doesn't crash the API (fail-open confirmed).
  • [ ] Two app instances share one counter.
Out of scope (unless asked)
  • IP-based limiting for unauthenticated routes.
  • Per-endpoint or per-tier limits.
  • Admin override or allowlist tooling.

Implementation Plan: Per-User Rate Limiting (Express + Redis)

Goal

Limit each authenticated user to 100 requests per 60 seconds. Over-limit requests get 429 Too Many Requests with a Retry-After header (seconds). State lives in Redis so limits hold across API instances.

Assumptions (verify in step 1)
  • JWT middleware runs before the limiter and sets req.user.id.
  • Node/Express project already using npm, yarn, or pnpm.
  • A Redis instance is available, URL supplied via env var.
Step 1: Reconnaissance (no changes)
  • Find the app entry point (app.use(...) registrations) and the JWT middleware. Note middleware order and which routes are protected.
  • Look for an existing Redis client (grep -r "redis" src package.json). Reuse it; don't open a second connection.
  • Identify the test framework, config/env-loading pattern, logger, and error-handling conventions. Follow them.
  • Check whether the app is behind a proxy/load balancer. This affects only the unauthenticated fallback (Step 4).
Step 2: Dependencies
  • If no Redis client exists, install ioredis or redis (node-redis v4+), matching codebase style. Default to ioredis if there's no precedent.
  • Do not add express-rate-limit or similar. A small custom limiter gives exact control over the Lua logic and headers.
  • Tests: prefer real Redis if CI provides it, since mocks emulate Lua poorly. Otherwise use ioredis-mock as a dev dependency.
Step 3: Configuration

Add to the config module and .env.example:

REDIS_URL=redis://localhost:6379
RATE_LIMIT_MAX=100
RATE_LIMIT_WINDOW_SECONDS=60
RATE_LIMIT_FAIL_OPEN=true

Defaults: 100 and 60. Parse as integers; fail fast on invalid values.

Step 4: Middleware

Create src/middleware/rateLimit.js (match project language and directory conventions, e.g. .ts).

Algorithm: fixed-window counter with an atomic Lua script. Known tradeoff: up to 2x burst at window boundaries. Implement fixed window by default; if the burst is unacceptable, a sliding-window log (sorted set) is the alternative. Note this in the PR description.

Key: ratelimit:{userId}:{windowStart}, where windowStart = floor(now / window) * window (epoch seconds). Retry-After = windowStart + window - now.

Atomic script (via EVAL; avoids an INCR/EXPIRE race where a crash between them leaves a key with no TTL):

local current = redis.call('INCR', KEYS[1])
if current == 1 then
  redis.call('EXPIRE', KEYS[1], ARGV[1])
end
return current

ARGV[1] = windowSeconds (optionally +1s buffer). Load once: defineCommand in ioredis, or SCRIPT LOAD/EVALSHA in node-redis.

Request flow:

  • Read req.user?.id. If missing, the limiter is mounted before auth or the route is public. Don't skip silently. Either:
  • (a) limit by req.ip, only if trust proxy is configured correctly; or
  • (b) call next() if the route is intentionally public.

If trust proxy is not configured correctly, log a warning and skip. Document the choice in a code comment.

  • Compute now, windowStart, and key; run the script to get count.
  • On every response set:
  • RateLimit-Limit: 100
  • RateLimit-Remaining: max(0, 100 - count)
  • RateLimit-Reset: <seconds until window end>
  • If count > limit: set Retry-After (integer, min 1), respond 429 using the project's error format (e.g. { "error": "Too Many Requests", "retryAfter": N }), and don't call next().
  • Otherwise call next().

Redis failure: fail open. Catch the error, log at error level with the user id, and call next(), so a Redis outage doesn't take down the API. Set a 200–500 ms connect/command timeout so a hung Redis adds no significant latency. Gate this on RATE_LIMIT_FAIL_OPEN so it can be flipped.

Export: createRateLimiter({ redis, limit, windowSeconds, now? }). The optional clock (now) enables testing. Instantiate with config values in app wiring.

Step 5: Wiring
  • Register after JWT middleware and before route handlers: app.use(jwtMiddleware); app.use(rateLimiter);. Or mount on the authenticated router if the app has public routes.
  • Exempt /health and /ready by mounting the limiter below them or via a path skip.
  • Close the Redis client in the existing graceful-shutdown handler, if there is one.
Step 6: Tests

Use the project's framework (supertest or similar). Cover:

  • Requests 1–100 in a window succeed, with decreasing RateLimit-Remaining.
  • Request 101 returns 429, with an integer Retry-After between 1 and 60 and the correct body.
  • Two users have independent counters.
  • Counter resets in a new window (fake timers or injected now()).
  • Redis failure passes the request through and logs an error.
  • Key has TTL > 0 after the first request.
  • Concurrency: ~150 parallel requests from one user yield exactly 100 successes.
Step 7: Documentation
  • Add the new env vars to .env.example and the README/API docs.
  • Document the 429 response shape and headers for API consumers.
Step 8: Verification
  • [ ] Existing tests pass.
  • [ ] New tests pass.
  • [ ] Manual: for i in $(seq 1 105); do curl -s -o /dev/null -w "%{http_code}\n" -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" localhost:3000/api/...; done shows 100 successes, then 429s.
  • [ ] redis-cli --scan --pattern 'ratelimit:*' shows keys with TTL ≤ 60.
  • [ ] Lint/type-check passes.
  • [ ] No new Redis connection if one already existed.
Out of Scope (mention in PR, don't build)
  • Per-route or per-tier limits.
  • Sliding-window precision.
  • IP limiting beyond the Step 4 fallback.
  • Metrics/dashboards. As a cheap start, log a structured line on each 429.

Plan: Per-User Rate Limiting (Express + Redis)

Goal

Limit each authenticated user to 100 requests per 60 seconds, backed by Redis. Over-limit requests get HTTP 429 with a Retry-After header (seconds). The existing JWT middleware sets req.user.id.

Design Decisions
  • Algorithm: Fixed-window counter in an atomic Lua script (INCR, EXPIRE on first hit, returns count and TTL). One Redis round trip, race-free. Tradeoff: a user can burst up to 2x at window boundaries. If that's unacceptable, use a sliding-window log or token bucket (see Optional upgrades).
  • Key: ratelimit:{userId}:{windowSeconds}, expiring after the window length.
  • Placement: After the JWT middleware, so req.user.id exists. Requests without it skip this middleware (auth should reject them, or a separate IP limiter handles them; out of scope).
  • Failure mode: Fail open on Redis errors (log, allow the request) so a Redis outage doesn't take down the API. Configurable.
  • Library: Reuse the project's Redis client if it has one; otherwise add ioredis. No rate-limit library: the logic is small and custom code gives exact header control.
Steps
1. Reconnaissance (no changes)
  • Find the Express app setup file (app.js/server.ts) and where the JWT middleware is mounted.
  • Check package.json for a Redis client (ioredis, redis) and an existing connection module/config. Reuse it.
  • Note language (JS/TS), module system (CJS/ESM), test framework, and lint/format conventions. Match them.
  • Note config conventions (dotenv, config module) and how REDIS_URL or equivalent is supplied.
2. Dependencies
  • If no Redis client exists: npm install ioredis (ships its own types).
  • For tests, prefer a real Redis if the repo already uses one in integration tests; some mocks (including possibly ioredis-mock) handle Lua poorly. Verify before using a mock.
3. Configuration

Follow the project's config pattern:

| Variable | Default | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| RATE_LIMIT_MAX | 100 | Requests per window |
| RATE_LIMIT_WINDOW_SECONDS | 60 | Window length |
| RATE_LIMIT_FAIL_OPEN | true | Allow requests on Redis error |
| REDIS_URL | existing | Redis connection |

4. Middleware

Create src/middleware/rateLimit.(js|ts) (match repo layout). Export createRateLimiter({ redis, max, windowSeconds, failOpen }), returning Express middleware.

Lua script:

local current = redis.call('INCR', KEYS[1])
if current == 1 then
  redis.call('EXPIRE', KEYS[1], ARGV[1])
end
local ttl = redis.call('TTL', KEYS[1])
if ttl < 0 then
  redis.call('EXPIRE', KEYS[1], ARGV[1])
  ttl = tonumber(ARGV[1])
end
return {current, ttl}

The ttl < 0 branch repairs a key that lost its expiry, which would otherwise lock the user out permanently.

Behavior:

  • If !req.user?.id, call next() and return.
  • Run the script (redis.defineCommand in ioredis, or eval/evalsha) with key ratelimit:${req.user.id}:${windowSeconds}.
  • Set on every response: RateLimit-Limit: <max>, RateLimit-Remaining: <max(0, max - current)>, RateLimit-Reset: <ttl>.
  • If current > max: set Retry-After: <ttl> (integer, minimum 1) and respond 429 with { "error": "Too Many Requests", "retryAfter": <ttl> }. Use the API's existing error shape if there is one.
  • Otherwise call next().
  • On Redis error: log with the project's logger; if failOpen, call next(), else respond 503.
5. Wire it in
  • Mount immediately after the JWT middleware, before route handlers:
  app.use(jwtMiddleware);
  app.use(createRateLimiter({ redis, max, windowSeconds, failOpen }));
  • If JWT is applied per-router, mount the limiter on those routers right after it.
  • Exempt health/metrics endpoints (e.g., /health) by mounting them before the limiter or via a skip list.
  • Proxy setup needs no change; the limit is per user, not per IP.
6. Tests

Use the project's framework (and supertest if already used):

  • Requests 1–100 in a window succeed; request 101 returns 429.
  • 429 includes Retry-After as an integer in 1–60.
  • RateLimit-Remaining decrements correctly.
  • Two users have independent counters.
  • After the window expires (1–2 second window in test config, or manipulate TTL), requests succeed again.
  • Requests without req.user bypass the limiter.
  • Redis failure: failOpen=true allows the request and logs an error; failOpen=false returns 503.
  • Concurrency: ~150 parallel requests for one user; exactly 100 succeed.
7. Documentation
  • README/API docs: limit, window, response headers, 429 format, new env vars.
  • Add the env vars to .env.example.
8. Verification
  • [ ] Existing tests pass.
  • [ ] New tests pass.
  • [ ] Lint/type-check passes.
  • [ ] Manual: send 101 curl requests with a valid JWT; the 101st returns 429 with Retry-After.
  • [ ] redis-cli TTL ratelimit:<id>:60 shows a positive TTL, and keys expire on their own.
Optional upgrades (do not implement unless asked)
  • Sliding-window log or token bucket to remove boundary bursts.
  • Per-route or per-tier limits (e.g., higher for paid users).
  • Separate IP-based limiter for unauthenticated endpoints such as login.
  • Redis Cluster: keys are single-key, so the script is cluster-safe; verify hash-slot behavior if clustering is used.
Constraints for the executing agent
  • Don't modify the JWT middleware.
  • Don't change existing route behavior beyond adding the limiter and headers.
  • Keep changes minimal and match existing style.
  • If an assumption here conflicts with the repo (different auth flow, no Redis config), stop and report the conflict instead of guessing.

It compares a `.env.example` file against a `.env` file and reports keys that are missing from `.env`, keys that are extra in `.env`, and keys that are present but empty. Exit code 0 if nothing is missing, 1 otherwise. A coding agent will implement it from your spec.

envcheck: Specification

Purpose

envcheck is a CLI tool that compares a .env.example file (the template) against a .env file (the actual config) and reports discrepancies. It is intended for local development and CI use.

Usage
envcheck [--example <path>] [--env <path>] [--strict] [--no-color]
envcheck --help
envcheck --version
Options

| Option | Default | Description |
|---|---|---|
| --example <path> | .env.example | Path to the template file |
| --env <path> | .env | Path to the actual env file |
| --strict | off | Also fail (exit 1) when extra or empty keys are found |
| --no-color | off | Disable ANSI colors. Colors are also disabled automatically if stdout is not a TTY or the NO_COLOR env var is set |
| --help, -h | | Print usage and exit 0 |
| --version, -v | | Print version and exit 0 |

Parsing Rules

Both files are parsed identically. Implement a small custom parser (do not require a third-party dotenv library) with these rules:

  • Split the file into lines (handle \n and \r\n). Strip a leading UTF-8 BOM if present.
  • Trim leading and trailing whitespace from each line.
  • Skip blank lines and lines starting with #.
  • Strip an optional leading export prefix (export followed by whitespace).
  • Split on the first =. The part before is the key (trimmed); the part after is the raw value.
  • A line with no = is treated as a key with an empty value (e.g. FOO is key FOO, value empty).
  • Keys must match ^[A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]*$. Lines with invalid keys are skipped and a warning is written to stderr: warning: <file>:<lineno>: invalid key, skipping.
  • Value handling:
  • Trim whitespace around the raw value.
  • If the value begins with " or ' and has a matching closing quote, the value is the content between the quotes (no escape processing needed). Anything after the closing quote, such as an inline comment, is ignored.
  • Otherwise, for an unquoted value, strip an inline comment: everything from the first # (whitespace followed by #) onward, then trim.
  • A value is empty if, after the above processing, it is the empty string. A value of "" or '' is empty. Whitespace-only quoted values (e.g. " ") are NOT empty.
  • If a key appears more than once in a file, the last occurrence wins. Do not warn.

Multi-line quoted values are out of scope. A quoted value with no closing quote on the same line is treated as an unquoted value.

Comparison Semantics

Let E be the set of keys in the example file and A the set of keys in the actual file.

  • Missing: keys in E but not in A.
  • Extra: keys in A but not in E.
  • Empty: keys in A (and also in E) whose value is empty. Keys that are missing are not also reported as empty. Extra keys with empty values are not reported as empty.

Note: whether the example file's own value is empty is irrelevant. An empty value in the example just means "you must supply this."

Output

Write the report to stdout. Sort keys alphabetically within each section. Omit sections that have no entries. Warnings and errors go to stderr.

Format:

Missing in .env (2):
  DATABASE_URL
  SECRET_KEY

Extra in .env (1):
  LEGACY_FLAG

Empty in .env (1):
  API_TOKEN
  • Section headers use the actual file path passed (or the default), e.g. Missing in ./config/.env (2):.
  • Colors (when enabled): Missing header in red, Extra in yellow, Empty in yellow. Key names uncolored.
  • If there are no issues at all, print ✓ .env matches .env.example (using the actual file names) and exit 0.
Exit Codes

| Code | Condition |
|---|---|
| 0 | No missing keys (and, in --strict mode, no extra or empty keys either) |
| 1 | One or more missing keys; or in --strict mode, any extra or empty keys |
| 2 | Usage or runtime error (unknown flag, file not found, unreadable file) |

Extra and empty keys are always reported but only affect the exit code under --strict.

Error Handling
  • If either file does not exist or cannot be read, print error: cannot read <path>: <reason> to stderr and exit 2.
  • Unknown flags: print error: unknown option <flag> and a hint to run --help, exit 2.
  • A missing value for an option that requires one (e.g. --env with no path): print an error, exit 2.
Implementation Notes
  • Language: implement in whatever the project's existing language is. If starting fresh, use Node.js (TypeScript) or Python 3 with no runtime dependencies beyond the standard library. Argument parsing may use the standard library.
  • Structure the code so that the parser and the comparison logic are pure functions (parseEnv(text) -> Map<string,string> and compare(example, actual) -> {missing, extra, empty}), separate from I/O, so they can be unit tested.
  • Never print values from either file, only key names. .env files contain secrets.
Test Cases

Include automated tests covering at least:

  • Identical files: exit 0, success message.
  • Missing key: reported, exit 1.
  • Extra key only: reported, exit 0 (exit 1 with --strict).
  • Empty value only (FOO=, FOO="", FOO=''): reported, exit 0 (exit 1 with --strict).
  • FOO=" " is not reported as empty.
  • Comments, blank lines, export FOO=bar, and CRLF line endings parse correctly.
  • Inline comment: FOO=bar # note yields value bar; FOO="a # b" yields a # b.
  • Value containing = (e.g. URL=postgres://h/db?a=b) parses correctly.
  • Duplicate keys: last wins (e.g. FOO=x then FOO= makes FOO empty).
  • Missing file: error on stderr, exit 2.
  • Invalid key line: skipped with a warning on stderr.
  • Output never contains any values from the files.
  • A key that is missing is not also listed under Empty.
Out of Scope

Variable interpolation (${VAR}), multi-line values, type or format validation, reading from the process environment, and auto-fixing or writing files.

envcheck: Specification

Purpose

envcheck is a CLI tool that compares a .env.example file (the template) against a .env file (the actual config). It reports:

  • Missing: keys in .env.example but absent from .env
  • Extra: keys in .env but absent from .env.example
  • Empty: keys present in .env with an empty value

It exits 0 if no keys are missing, 1 otherwise.

Usage
envcheck [--example PATH] [--env PATH] [--strict] [--no-color]

| Flag | Default | Description |
|---|---|---|
| --example PATH | .env.example | Path to the template file |
| --env PATH | .env | Path to the actual file |
| --strict | off | Also fail (exit 1) if extra or empty keys are found |
| --no-color | off | Disable ANSI colors (also disabled if stdout is not a TTY or NO_COLOR env var is set) |
| -h, --help | | Print usage and exit 0 |

Parsing Rules

Both files are parsed identically. Read as UTF-8. Strip a leading BOM if present.

For each line:

  • Strip leading and trailing whitespace.
  • Skip blank lines and lines starting with #.
  • Strip an optional leading export prefix (export followed by whitespace).
  • Split on the first =. Left side is the key, right side is the value.
  • Trim whitespace around the key. Lines with no = or an empty key are ignored (but see Warnings).
  • Trim whitespace around the value. If the value begins with " or ', and the matching quote appears later in the value, the value is the content between them (inline comments after the closing quote are discarded). Otherwise, an unquoted value ends at the first # (space followed by hash), which begins an inline comment; strip it and trailing whitespace.
  • A value is empty if, after the processing above, it is the zero-length string. A quoted empty string (KEY="") is empty. A value of only whitespace inside quotes (KEY=" ") is not empty.

Valid keys match [A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_.]*. Lines whose key does not match are ignored and produce a warning on stderr: warning: <file>:<line>: ignored invalid line.

Duplicate keys: the last occurrence wins. Emit a warning on stderr: warning: <file>:<line>: duplicate key <KEY>.

Multi-line values are not supported. Each line is parsed independently.

Variable interpolation (${VAR}) is not performed; values are treated as literal text.

Comparison Logic

Let E = set of keys in the example, A = set of keys in the actual file.

  • missing = E − A
  • extra = A − E
  • empty = keys in A whose value is empty. Report each empty key regardless of whether the example's value is also empty. (Keep it simple; no special-casing.)

All lists are sorted alphabetically (byte order) in output.

Output

Written to stdout. Sections appear in this order and are omitted if empty:

Missing (2):
  DATABASE_URL
  SECRET_KEY

Extra (1):
  OLD_FLAG

Empty (1):
  API_TOKEN

If all three lists are empty, print:

OK: .env matches .env.example

(Use the actual paths passed in, not hardcoded names.)

Colors (when enabled): section header for Missing in red, Extra in yellow, Empty in yellow. "OK" line in green.

Never print values, only key names, so secrets are not leaked into logs.

Exit Codes

| Code | Condition |
|---|---|
| 0 | No missing keys (and, with --strict, no extra or empty keys) |
| 1 | Missing keys exist (or, with --strict, any extra or empty keys exist) |
| 2 | Usage or I/O error: unreadable file, file not found, bad flags |

Errors go to stderr in the form envcheck: error: <message>. If either file does not exist, name the missing file in the message.

Implementation Notes
  • Language: choose one that produces a single easy-to-run artifact. Python 3.8+ with only the standard library (argparse, sys, os, re) is preferred. Provide it as a single file envcheck.py with a main() entry point and an if __name__ == "__main__": guard.
  • Separate the parser (parse_env(text, filename) -> (dict, warnings)) and comparison (compare(example, actual) -> (missing, extra, empty)) into pure functions so they can be unit tested.
  • No third-party dependencies.
Tests

Include a test_envcheck.py using unittest or pytest covering at minimum:

  • Missing, extra, and empty detection each in isolation
  • All three together
  • Matching files produce the OK message and exit 0
  • Extra/empty only: exit 0 normally, exit 1 with --strict
  • Comments, blank lines, export prefix, quoted values, inline comments
  • KEY="" counts as empty; KEY=" " does not
  • Value containing = (e.g. URL=a=b) parses correctly
  • Duplicate keys and invalid lines produce warnings
  • Missing file produces exit code 2 and an error naming the file
  • Values never appear in output
Out of Scope
  • Multi-line values, variable interpolation, auto-fixing or generating .env
  • Type or format validation of values
  • Scanning multiple env files or directories
