envcheck: Specification

Purpose

envcheck is a CLI tool that compares a .env.example file (the template) against a .env file (the actual config) and reports discrepancies. It is intended for local development and CI use.

Usage
envcheck [--example PATH] [--env PATH] [--strict] [--quiet] [--no-color]
envcheck --help
envcheck --version
Options

| Flag | Default | Description |
|---|---|---|
| --example PATH | .env.example | Path to the template file |
| --env PATH | .env | Path to the actual file |
| --strict | off | Also fail (exit 1) on extra keys and empty keys |
| --quiet, -q | off | Suppress all output; only the exit code is meaningful |
| --no-color | off | Disable ANSI colors. Colors are also disabled when stdout is not a TTY or when the NO_COLOR env var is set |
| --help, -h | | Print usage and exit 0 |
| --version | | Print version and exit 0 |

Definitions
  • Missing: key present in the example file but absent from the env file.
  • Extra: key present in the env file but absent from the example file.
  • Empty: key present in the env file with an empty value (after parsing; see below). Only keys that exist in the env file are checked. A missing key is not also reported as empty.
Parsing Rules

Both files use the same parser. Implement a small hand-written parser; do not require a third-party dotenv library.

  • Read as UTF-8. Strip a leading BOM if present. Accept both \n and \r\n line endings.
  • Blank lines and lines whose first non-whitespace character is # are ignored.
  • An optional leading export (followed by whitespace) is stripped.
  • A line is KEY=VALUE, split on the first =. Whitespace around the key is trimmed. Lines with no = are ignored but produce a warning on stderr (warning: <file>:<line>: ignoring malformed line).
  • Valid keys match [A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]*. Lines with invalid keys are treated as malformed (same warning).
  • Value handling:
  • Trim leading and trailing whitespace from the raw value.
  • If the value starts with " or ', read to the matching closing quote; the content between is the value. Anything after the closing quote is ignored (this includes trailing comments). Do not process escape sequences. An unterminated quote takes the rest of the line as the value.
  • Otherwise, an unquoted value ends at the first # (whitespace followed by #); the remainder is a comment and is trimmed off.
  • A value is empty if the result is the empty string. KEY="" and KEY= are both empty. KEY=" " is not empty (whitespace inside quotes is preserved).
  • Values are never printed by the tool, as they may be secrets. Only key names appear in output.
  • If a key appears more than once in a file, the last occurrence wins for emptiness; the key is reported only once.
Output

Report to stdout, grouped by category, with keys sorted alphabetically within each group. Omit groups with no entries. Example:

Missing in .env (2):
  DATABASE_URL
  SECRET_KEY

Extra in .env (1):
  LEGACY_FLAG

Empty in .env (1):
  API_TOKEN
  • Group headers use the actual paths passed (or defaults), e.g. Missing in config/.env (2):.
  • If there are no issues of any kind, print OK: .env matches .env.example (with actual paths) to stdout.
  • Colors (when enabled): Missing in red, Extra in yellow, Empty in yellow.
  • Warnings and errors go to stderr, never stdout.
  • With --quiet, print nothing to stdout; warnings and errors on stderr are also suppressed except for fatal errors (exit 2).
Exit Codes

| Code | Meaning |
|---|---|
| 0 | No missing keys (and, with --strict, no extra or empty keys either) |
| 1 | One or more missing keys (or, with --strict, any extra or empty keys) |
| 2 | Usage or I/O error: unknown flag, file not found, unreadable file, or a path that is a directory |

Extra and empty keys are always reported regardless of --strict; the flag only changes the exit code.

Error message format on exit 2: envcheck: error: <description> on stderr, e.g. envcheck: error: cannot read .env: no such file or directory.

Implementation Notes
  • Language: implementer's choice, but it should compile to or run as a single self-contained executable with no runtime dependencies beyond the standard library (Go, Rust, or Python with stdlib only are all fine). If unsure, use Python 3.9+ with argparse.
  • Do not follow or interpret variable interpolation (${VAR}); treat values as literal text.
  • Do not read from or modify the process environment.
  • Keep the code in a structure that separates parsing, comparison, and reporting, so each can be unit-tested.
Test Cases

The implementation must include tests covering at least:

  • Identical files → OK, exit 0.
  • Key missing from env → listed under Missing, exit 1.
  • Extra key in env only → listed under Extra, exit 0 (exit 1 with --strict).
  • KEY= and KEY="" → reported as Empty, exit 0 (exit 1 with --strict).
  • KEY=" " → not reported as empty.
  • export KEY=value, CRLF line endings, and a BOM are parsed correctly.
  • KEY=value # comment → value is value; KEY="a # b" → value is a # b.
  • Value containing = (e.g. URL=postgres://u:p@h/db?x=1) is parsed correctly.
  • Duplicate keys are reported once; last occurrence determines emptiness.
  • Malformed line → warning on stderr, parsing continues.
  • Missing .env file → exit 2 with error on stderr.
  • Missing key is not also listed as Empty.
  • --quiet produces no stdout in all cases.
  • Secret values never appear in any output.
Out of Scope

Type validation, value format checks, multiline values, variable interpolation, multiple env files, and auto-fixing or generating .env from the example.

envcheck spec

Purpose

CLI that compares .env.example (reference) to .env (actual) and reports drift. Single-file implementation, no runtime dependencies beyond the language's standard library.

Usage
envcheck [--example PATH] [--env PATH] [--no-color]
  • --example PATH: default .env.example
  • --env PATH: default .env
  • --no-color: disable ANSI color. Color is also off when stdout is not a TTY or NO_COLOR is set.
  • -h, --help: print usage, exit 0.
Parsing rules

Apply to both files identically.

  • Read as UTF-8. Strip a leading BOM.
  • Split on \n; strip trailing \r.
  • Skip blank lines and lines whose first non-whitespace char is #.
  • Strip an optional leading export (with whitespace) before the key.
  • Split on the first =. Left side, trimmed, is the key. Lines with no = are ignored.
  • A key must match [A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]*. Lines with invalid keys are ignored and produce a warning on stderr: warn: <file>:<line>: invalid key.
  • Value is the right side, trimmed. If wrapped in matching single or double quotes, strip them. Do not process escapes or inline comments; value is otherwise taken literally.
  • A value is empty if it is the empty string after the trimming and unquoting above. Whitespace-only quoted values (" ") are not empty.
  • Duplicate keys: last occurrence wins. Emit warn: <file>:<line>: duplicate key <KEY> to stderr.
  • No variable interpolation. No multiline values.
Comparison

Let E = keys in example, A = keys in actual.

  • missing = E − A. Keys in the example absent from .env.
  • extra = A − E. Keys in .env absent from the example.
  • empty = keys in A whose value is empty. Reported regardless of whether the example's value is empty, except keys in E whose example value is also empty are skipped (the example signals the key is optional/blank by design).

Each list is sorted alphabetically (byte order).

Output

Written to stdout. Sections appear only if non-empty, in this order: missing, extra, empty.

Missing (2):
  DATABASE_URL
  SECRET_KEY

Extra (1):
  LEGACY_FLAG

Empty (1):
  API_TOKEN
  • Headers colored red (missing), yellow (extra), yellow (empty) when color is on.
  • If all three lists are empty, print OK: .env matches .env.example and nothing else.
  • Sections are separated by one blank line; no trailing blank line.
Exit codes
  • 0: missing list is empty. Extra and empty keys alone do not fail the check, but are still reported.
  • 1: missing list is non-empty.
  • 2: usage error or unreadable file (not found, permission denied). Message to stderr: error: cannot read <path>: <reason>.
Edge cases
  • Either file missing: exit 2, no report.
  • Empty file: valid, zero keys.
  • Same file passed for both: reports OK.
Tests

Implement tests covering at minimum:

  • Missing only → exit 1.
  • Extra only → exit 0, reported.
  • Empty only → exit 0, reported.
  • Example key with blank value, .env also blank → not reported as empty.
  • Quoted empty (KEY="") → empty; KEY=" " → not empty.
  • export KEY=val, CRLF line endings, BOM, comments.
  • Duplicate key warning, last wins.
  • Invalid key line warns and is ignored.
  • Missing file → exit 2.
  • All matching → OK line, exit 0.
Non-goals

Value comparison, type validation, .env writing or fixing, multiple env files, JSON output.

envcheck spec

Purpose

CLI that compares .env.example (reference) against .env (actual) and reports drift. Single binary/script, no network, no dependencies beyond the language stdlib.

Usage
envcheck [--example PATH] [--env PATH] [--strict]
  • --example PATH: default .env.example
  • --env PATH: default .env
  • --strict: also fail (exit 1) on extra and empty keys. Without it, only missing keys fail.
  • -h/--help: print usage, exit 0.
Parsing rules

Apply identically to both files.

  • One entry per line: KEY=VALUE.
  • Skip blank lines and lines whose first non-whitespace char is #.
  • Strip an optional leading export before the key.
  • Split on the first = only. Trim whitespace around the key. Key must match [A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]*; lines that don't match are ignored with a warning to stderr (warn: <file>:<line>: unparseable).
  • Value: trim surrounding whitespace. If wrapped in matching single or double quotes, strip them. Unquoted values end at an unquoted # (inline comment); strip it.
  • A value is empty if it is the empty string after the steps above (KEY=, KEY="", KEY='').
  • Duplicate keys: last one wins. No warning.
  • Multiline values are not supported.
  • Do not expand variables or read the process environment.
Definitions
  • missing: key in example, not in env.
  • extra: key in env, not in example.
  • empty: key in env with an empty value. Report this only if the key is also in example (an empty extra key is reported only as extra).

Whether the example value is empty does not matter; the check looks only at .env.

Output

Write to stdout. Sort keys alphabetically within each section. Omit sections with no entries. Format:

missing (2):
  API_KEY
  DB_URL
extra (1):
  LEGACY_FLAG
empty (1):
  SECRET

If all three are empty, print ok and nothing else. Never print values.

Warnings and errors go to stderr. No color, no flags for it.

Exit codes
  • 0: no missing keys (and, with --strict, no extra or empty keys).
  • 1: otherwise.
  • 2: usage error, or either file is unreadable or does not exist. Print error: <message> to stderr.

Extra and empty keys are still printed without --strict; they just don't affect the exit code.

Tests

Cover at minimum:

  • Identical files → ok, exit 0.
  • Missing key → listed, exit 1.
  • Extra key only → listed, exit 0; with --strict, exit 1.
  • Empty forms: K=, K="", K='', K= all count as empty.
  • Empty extra key appears under extra only.
  • Comments, blank lines, export prefix, quoted values, inline # comment.
  • Value containing = (URL=a=b) parses as a=b.
  • Duplicate key, last wins (K=1 then K= → empty).
  • Missing file → exit 2.
  • Output never contains a value.

envcheck spec

Purpose

CLI that compares .env.example (reference) against .env (actual) and reports drift. Single-file implementation, no runtime dependencies beyond the language's standard library.

Usage
envcheck [--example PATH] [--env PATH]
  • --example PATH: default .env.example
  • --env PATH: default .env
  • -h, --help: print usage, exit 0

Unknown flags: print usage to stderr, exit 2.

Parsing rules

Apply identically to both files.

  • Read as UTF-8. Strip a leading BOM if present.
  • Split on \n; strip a trailing \r from each line.
  • Skip blank lines and lines whose first non-whitespace character is #.
  • Strip an optional leading export (export followed by whitespace).
  • Split on the first =. Key is the left side, trimmed. Value is the right side, trimmed.
  • Skip lines with no = or an empty key. Emit a warning to stderr: warn: <file>:<line>: unparseable line.
  • Value handling: if the trimmed value is wrapped in matching " or ', remove the quotes. Otherwise, strip an inline comment starting at the first # (space then hash). No escape processing or variable expansion.
  • A value is empty if it is the empty string after the steps above. Whitespace-only quoted values (" ") are not empty.
  • Duplicate keys: last one wins.
Comparison

Let E = keys in example, A = keys in actual.

  • missing = E − A (in example, absent from .env)
  • extra = A − E (in .env, not in example)
  • empty = keys in A whose value is empty. Report only keys that are also in E; empty extras are already reported as extra.

Within each category, sort keys alphabetically (byte order).

If a key is empty in the example and also empty in .env, it is still reported as empty. (The example's blank is a placeholder; the user is expected to fill it in.)

Output

Written to stdout. Omit any section with zero entries. Format:

Missing (2):
  DATABASE_URL
  SECRET_KEY

Extra (1):
  LEGACY_FLAG

Empty (1):
  API_TOKEN

Sections appear in the order Missing, Extra, Empty, separated by one blank line. If all three are empty, print OK and nothing else.

No color, no ANSI codes. Never print values.

Exit codes
  • 0: no keys missing and no keys empty. Extra keys alone do not cause failure.
  • 1: one or more keys missing or empty.
  • 2: usage error, or a file cannot be read. For an unreadable file, print error: cannot read <path>: <reason> to stderr.

Both files are checked for readability before any output is produced.

Edge cases
  • Empty .env.example: every key in .env is extra; exit 0.
  • Empty .env: every example key is missing; exit 1.
  • Same path passed for both: valid, yields OK.
Tests

Cover at minimum: each category in isolation, all three together, export prefix, quoted values, inline comments, duplicate keys, CRLF input, BOM, missing file (exit 2), and exit codes for extra-only (0) versus empty-only (1).

envcheck: Specification

envcheck is a CLI that compares a template .env.example against an actual .env and reports discrepancies. For local development and CI.

Usage
envcheck [--example <path>] [--env <path>] [--strict] [--no-color]
envcheck --help
envcheck --version

| Option | Default | Description |
|---|---|---|
| --example <path> | .env.example | Template file |
| --env <path> | .env | Actual env file |
| --strict | off | Also exit 1 on extra or empty keys |
| --no-color | off | Disable ANSI colors. Also disabled when stdout is not a TTY or NO_COLOR is set |
| --help, -h | | Print usage, exit 0 |
| --version, -v | | Print version, exit 0 |

Parsing

Both files use the same custom parser (no third-party dotenv library):

  • Split into lines on \n or \r\n. Strip a leading UTF-8 BOM.
  • Trim each line.
  • Skip blank lines and lines starting with #.
  • Strip a leading export followed by whitespace.
  • Split on the first =: left is the key (trimmed), right is the raw value.
  • A line with no = is a key with empty value (FOO → key FOO, value empty).
  • Keys must match ^[A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]*$. Otherwise skip the line and write to stderr: warning: <file>:<lineno>: invalid key, skipping.
  • Values:
  • Trim the raw value.
  • If it starts with " or ' and has a matching closing quote on the same line, the value is the content between the quotes (no escape processing). Anything after the closing quote is ignored.
  • Otherwise (including a quote with no closing quote on the line), it is unquoted: strip from the first whitespace-then-# onward, then trim.
  • A value is empty if the result is the empty string. "" and '' are empty. " " is not.
  • Duplicate keys: last wins, no warning.

Multi-line quoted values are out of scope.

Comparison

E = keys in the example, A = keys in the actual file.

  • Missing: in E, not in A.
  • Extra: in A, not in E.
  • Empty: in both E and A, with an empty value in A. Missing keys and extra keys are never reported as empty.

The example file's own values are irrelevant; an empty example value means "you must supply this."

Output

Report to stdout; warnings and errors to stderr. Sort keys alphabetically within each section and omit empty sections.

Missing in .env (2):
  DATABASE_URL
  SECRET_KEY

Extra in .env (1):
  LEGACY_FLAG

Empty in .env (1):
  API_TOKEN
  • Headers use the actual paths passed (or defaults), e.g. Missing in ./config/.env (2):.
  • With colors: Missing header red, Extra and Empty headers yellow, key names uncolored.
  • No issues: print ✓ .env matches .env.example (using actual file names), exit 0.
Exit Codes

| Code | Condition |
|---|---|
| 0 | No missing keys (and, with --strict, no extra or empty keys) |
| 1 | Any missing key; with --strict, also any extra or empty key |
| 2 | Usage or runtime error |

Extra and empty keys are always reported but affect the exit code only under --strict.

Errors (exit 2)
  • Unreadable or nonexistent file: error: cannot read <path>: <reason> to stderr.
  • Unknown flag: error: unknown option <flag> plus a hint to run --help.
  • Option missing its required value (e.g. --env with no path): print an error.
Implementation Notes
  • Use the project's existing language. If starting fresh, use Node.js (TypeScript) or Python 3, standard library only (argument parsing may use it).
  • Keep parseEnv(text) -> Map<string,string> and compare(example, actual) -> {missing, extra, empty} pure and separate from I/O for unit testing.
  • Never print values from either file, only key names. .env files contain secrets.
Tests

Automated tests must cover at least:

  • Identical files: exit 0, success message.
  • Missing key: reported, exit 1.
  • Extra key only: reported, exit 0 (1 with --strict).
  • Empty value only (FOO=, FOO="", FOO=''): reported, exit 0 (1 with --strict).
  • FOO=" " is not reported as empty.
  • Comments, blank lines, export FOO=bar, and CRLF parse correctly.
  • FOO=bar # note → bar; FOO="a # b" → a # b.
  • Value containing = (URL=postgres://h/db?a=b) parses correctly.
  • Duplicates: last wins (FOO=x then FOO= makes FOO empty).
  • Missing file: error on stderr, exit 2.
  • Invalid key line: skipped, warning on stderr.
  • Output never contains values from the files.
  • A missing key is not also listed under Empty.
Out of Scope

Variable interpolation (${VAR}), multi-line values, type or format validation, reading the process environment, auto-fixing or writing files.

envcheck: Specification

envcheck is a CLI tool that compares a template file (.env.example) against an actual config file (.env) and reports:

  • Missing: keys in the example but not in the actual file
  • Extra: keys in the actual file but not in the example
  • Empty: keys in the actual file with an empty value
Usage
envcheck [--example PATH] [--env PATH] [--strict] [--no-color]

| Flag | Default | Description |
|---|---|---|
| --example PATH | .env.example | Template file |
| --env PATH | .env | Actual file |
| --strict | off | Also exit 1 on extra or empty keys |
| --no-color | off | Disable ANSI colors. Also disabled when stdout is not a TTY or NO_COLOR is set |
| -h, --help | | Print usage, exit 0 |

Parsing

Both files are parsed identically. Read as UTF-8; strip a leading BOM.

For each line:

  • Strip leading and trailing whitespace.
  • Skip blank lines and lines starting with #.
  • Strip an optional export prefix (export followed by whitespace).
  • Split on the first =: left is the key, right is the value.
  • Trim whitespace around key and value. Lines with no = or an empty key are ignored.
  • Value handling:
  • If it starts with " or ' and the matching quote appears later, the value is the content between the quotes. Anything after the closing quote (e.g. an inline comment) is discarded.
  • Otherwise the value ends at the first # (space, hash); drop that comment and trailing whitespace.
  • A value is empty only if it is zero-length after processing. KEY="" is empty; KEY=" " is not.

Valid keys match [A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_.]*. Lines with an invalid key are ignored and warn on stderr: warning: <file>:<line>: ignored invalid line.

Duplicate keys: the last occurrence wins, with a stderr warning: warning: <file>:<line>: duplicate key <KEY>.

Multi-line values and ${VAR} interpolation are not supported. Each line is parsed independently and values are literal.

Comparison

Let E = keys in the example, A = keys in the actual file.

  • missing = E − A
  • extra = A − E
  • empty = keys in A with an empty value, regardless of the example's value

Output lists are sorted in byte order.

Output (stdout)

Sections appear in this order and are omitted when empty:

Missing (2):
  DATABASE_URL
  SECRET_KEY

Extra (1):
  OLD_FLAG

Empty (1):
  API_TOKEN

If all three are empty, print (using the actual paths passed, not hardcoded names):

OK: .env matches .env.example

Colors (when enabled): Missing header red; Extra and Empty headers yellow; OK line green.

Print only key names, never values, so secrets don't leak into logs.

Exit Codes

| Code | Condition |
|---|---|
| 0 | No missing keys (with --strict: also no extra or empty keys) |
| 1 | Missing keys exist (with --strict: or any extra or empty keys) |
| 2 | Usage or I/O error: unreadable or nonexistent file, bad flags |

Errors go to stderr as envcheck: error: <message>. If a file does not exist, the message names it.

Implementation
  • Single file envcheck.py, Python 3.8+, standard library only (argparse, sys, os, re), with main() and an if __name__ == "__main__": guard.
  • Pure, unit-testable functions: parse_env(text, filename) -> (dict, warnings) and compare(example, actual) -> (missing, extra, empty).
Tests

test_envcheck.py (unittest or pytest) must cover at minimum:

  • Missing, extra, and empty detection, each in isolation and all together
  • Matching files: OK message, exit 0
  • Extra/empty only: exit 0 normally, exit 1 with --strict
  • Comments, blank lines, export prefix, quoted values, inline comments
  • KEY="" is empty; KEY=" " is not
  • URL=a=b parses with value a=b
  • Duplicate keys and invalid lines produce warnings
  • Missing file: exit 2, error names the file
  • Values never appear in output
Out of Scope
  • Multi-line values, interpolation, auto-fixing or generating .env
  • Type or format validation of values
  • Scanning multiple files or directories

sample 2

envcheck: Specification

envcheck is a CLI tool for local development and CI. It compares a template file (.env.example) against an actual config file (.env) and reports discrepancies.

Usage
envcheck [--example PATH] [--env PATH] [--strict] [--quiet] [--no-color]
envcheck --help
envcheck --version

| Flag | Default | Description |
|---|---|---|
| --example PATH | .env.example | Template file |
| --env PATH | .env | Actual file |
| --strict | off | Exit 1 on extra and empty keys too |
| --quiet, -q | off | Suppress all stdout; only the exit code matters |
| --no-color | off | Disable ANSI colors. Colors are also off when stdout is not a TTY or NO_COLOR is set |
| --help, -h | | Print usage, exit 0 |
| --version | | Print version, exit 0 |

Definitions
  • Missing: key in the example file, absent from the env file.
  • Extra: key in the env file, absent from the example file.
  • Empty: key in the env file whose parsed value is empty. Only keys present in the env file are checked, so a missing key is never also reported as empty.
Parsing Rules

Both files use the same hand-written parser. No third-party dotenv library.

  • Read as UTF-8. Strip a leading BOM. Accept \n and \r\n.
  • Ignore blank lines and lines whose first non-whitespace character is #.
  • Strip an optional leading export (followed by whitespace).
  • Split KEY=VALUE on the first =. Trim whitespace around the key. A line with no = is ignored with a stderr warning: warning: <file>:<line>: ignoring malformed line.
  • Valid keys match [A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]*. A line with an invalid key is malformed (same warning).
  • Values:
  • Trim leading and trailing whitespace from the raw value.
  • If the value starts with " or ', the value is the content up to the matching closing quote. Ignore anything after the closing quote, including comments. Process no escape sequences. An unterminated quote takes the rest of the line.
  • Otherwise the value ends at the first # (whitespace then #); the comment is trimmed off.
  • A value is empty if the result is the empty string. KEY= and KEY="" are empty. KEY=" " is not (whitespace inside quotes is preserved).
  • Never print values (they may be secrets). Output contains key names only.
  • For a duplicate key, the last occurrence determines emptiness. The key is reported once.
Output

Print to stdout, grouped by category, keys sorted alphabetically within each group. Omit empty groups.

Missing in .env (2):
  DATABASE_URL
  SECRET_KEY

Extra in .env (1):
  LEGACY_FLAG

Empty in .env (1):
  API_TOKEN
  • Headers use the actual paths passed (or defaults), e.g. Missing in config/.env (2):.
  • With no issues, print OK: .env matches .env.example (actual paths) to stdout.
  • Colors, when enabled: Missing red, Extra yellow, Empty yellow.
  • Warnings and errors go to stderr, never stdout.
  • With --quiet, print nothing to stdout and suppress stderr warnings. Fatal errors (exit 2) are still printed.
Exit Codes

| Code | Meaning |
|---|---|
| 0 | No missing keys (with --strict, also no extra or empty keys) |
| 1 | Any missing key (with --strict, also any extra or empty key) |
| 2 | Usage or I/O error: unknown flag, file not found, unreadable file, or path is a directory |

Extra and empty keys are always reported; --strict only changes the exit code.

Exit-2 errors go to stderr as envcheck: error: <description>, e.g. envcheck: error: cannot read .env: no such file or directory.

Implementation Notes
  • Language is your choice, but the result must be a single self-contained executable or script using only the standard library (Go, Rust, or Python stdlib only). If unsure, use Python 3.9+ with argparse.
  • Treat values as literal text. Do not interpolate ${VAR}.
  • Do not read or modify the process environment.
  • Separate parsing, comparison, and reporting so each is unit-testable.
Required Tests
  • Identical files → OK, exit 0.
  • Key missing from env → under Missing, exit 1.
  • Extra key in env only → under Extra, exit 0 (1 with --strict).
  • KEY= and KEY="" → Empty, exit 0 (1 with --strict).
  • KEY=" " → not Empty.
  • export KEY=value, CRLF endings, and a BOM parse correctly.
  • KEY=value # comment → value; KEY="a # b" → a # b.
  • A value containing = (e.g. URL=postgres://u:p@h/db?x=1) parses correctly.
  • Duplicate keys are reported once; the last occurrence determines emptiness.
  • Malformed line → stderr warning, parsing continues.
  • Missing .env → exit 2, error on stderr.
  • A missing key is not also listed as Empty.
  • --quiet produces no stdout in any case.
  • Secret values never appear in any output.
Out of Scope

Type validation, value format checks, multiline values, variable interpolation, multiple env files, and auto-fixing or generating .env from the example.

s5a

Compare Postgres LISTEN/NOTIFY, Redis Pub/Sub, and Kafka for a notification service handling about 1,000 events per second, where losing an occasional event is acceptable and we already run Postgres and Redis. Recommend one.
baseline
sample 0

Recommendation: Redis Pub/Sub, unless you need consumers to catch up after downtime, in which case use Redis Streams (same infrastructure, still no new system).

Comparison at ~1,000 events/sec

Postgres LISTEN/NOTIFY

  • Throughput: 1k/sec is feasible, but NOTIFY takes a global lock at commit time, so concurrent writers serialize. It works at this scale and becomes a bottleneck as you grow.
  • Payload limit of 8KB; you'd typically send an ID and fetch the row.
  • Delivery: at-most-once, and only to currently connected listeners. A slow or stalled listener can cause the notification queue to fill, which can eventually make NOTIFY fail for everyone.
  • Each listener needs a dedicated connection, which competes with your pool and PgBouncer (transaction pooling breaks LISTEN).
  • Upside: transactional. The notification fires only if the transaction commits, which is valuable if events derive from DB writes.
  • Risk: adds load to your primary database, which is probably your most precious resource.

Redis Pub/Sub

  • Throughput: trivially handles 1k/sec; it can do 100k+/sec on modest hardware.
  • Latency: sub-millisecond.
  • Delivery: at-most-once, fire-and-forget. If a subscriber is disconnected, it misses messages. No persistence or replay.
  • Fan-out is simple and cheap. Operationally, there's nothing new to learn.
  • Downside: not transactional with Postgres writes, so you can publish an event for a transaction that later rolls back, or lose one if the app crashes between commit and publish. You said occasional loss is acceptable, so this is fine.

Kafka

  • Throughput: vastly more than you need.
  • Delivery: durable, replayable, consumer groups, ordering per partition. The strongest guarantees of the three.
  • Cost: a new system to deploy, monitor, upgrade, and understand (brokers, partitions, consumer lag, possibly KRaft/ZooKeeper). For 1k/sec with loss tolerated, you'd be paying for guarantees you've said you don't need.
Why Redis
  • You already run it, so there's no new operational burden.
  • It keeps notification load off Postgres.
  • Loss tolerance removes Kafka's main advantage.
  • It has plenty of headroom (100x) beyond your load.
Caveats and when to choose differently
  • Need catch-up after consumer restarts? Use Redis Streams with consumer groups. You get persistence, acknowledgments, and replay, with modest added complexity.
  • Events must be tied atomically to DB transactions? Use LISTEN/NOTIFY, or better, a transactional outbox table polled or relayed to Redis.
  • Expect to grow 10-100x, need multiple independent consumers replaying history, or need an event log as a system of record? Kafka becomes justified then, not now.
  • Redis Pub/Sub with many slow subscribers can bloat output buffers on the server. Set client-output-buffer-limit pubsub appropriately and monitor it.
  • If Redis is a single instance, a failover drops all subscriptions. Make sure clients reconnect and resubscribe automatically.
454 words · 658 tok_est · out 1101 · 10.3s · 4.57 overall
cor 5 cla 5 amb 4 sca 5 rel 4 con 4 cmp 5
facts 5/5
sample 1

Recommendation: Redis Pub/Sub, or Redis Streams if you want a safety net. Details below.

Comparison at ~1,000 events/sec

Postgres LISTEN/NOTIFY

  • Throughput: 1k/sec is feasible, but NOTIFY takes a global lock at commit time, so it serializes commits across the database. That can hurt your primary write workload as volume grows.
  • Payload limit of 8 KB, so you'll mostly send IDs and re-query.
  • Delivery: at-most-once, and listeners must hold a persistent connection (awkward behind PgBouncer in transaction mode).
  • Upside: transactional. The notification fires only if the transaction commits, which is a real correctness benefit.
  • Risk: you're putting notification load on your system of record.

Redis Pub/Sub

  • Throughput: 1k/sec is trivial; Redis handles 100k+/sec on modest hardware.
  • Latency: sub-millisecond.
  • Delivery: at-most-once, fire-and-forget. Disconnected subscribers miss messages. Since you can tolerate occasional loss, this fits.
  • Operationally: you already run it, there's no new infrastructure, and client libraries are mature.
  • Downsides: no persistence, no replay, no consumer groups (every subscriber gets every message, so horizontal scaling of workers means duplicate work unless you shard channels).

Kafka

  • Throughput: far beyond what you need.
  • Durability, replay, consumer groups, and ordering per partition are all excellent.
  • But you'd be adding a new system (brokers, KRaft/ZooKeeper, monitoring, upgrades, schema management) to solve problems you've said you don't have. At 1k/sec with loss tolerated, it's overkill.
Why Redis
  • Loss is acceptable, so Kafka's durability isn't buying you anything.
  • It avoids adding load or lock contention to Postgres.
  • It's already in your stack, so operational cost is near zero.
  • Performance headroom is about 100x over your requirement.
Caveats and a middle path
  • If you need multiple workers sharing the load (e.g., a pool of senders for push/email), plain Pub/Sub will deliver each event to all of them. Use Redis Streams with consumer groups instead. You get load-balanced consumption, acknowledgments, and short-term replay, still on infrastructure you already run, and it handles 1k/sec easily.
  • If events originate from DB writes and must match committed state, consider writing to an outbox table in the same transaction and having a small relay publish to Redis. That gives you transactional consistency without relying on NOTIFY.
  • When to revisit Kafka: if you later need long retention, replay for new consumers, multiple independent downstream consumers at scale, or strict ordering and durability guarantees.

Bottom line: Start with Redis Pub/Sub if every subscriber needs every event (e.g., fanning out to WebSocket gateways). Choose Redis Streams if work needs to be distributed across a worker pool. Skip Kafka for now, and avoid LISTEN/NOTIFY at this volume unless transactional coupling is your top priority.

448 words · 638 tok_est · out 1078 · 9.9s · 4.43 overall
cor 5 cla 5 amb 4 sca 4 rel 4 con 4 cmp 5
facts 5/5
sample 2

Recommendation: Redis Pub/Sub, unless you need subscribers to survive disconnects, in which case use Redis Streams (same infrastructure, small API change).

Comparison at ~1,000 events/sec, loss tolerated

Postgres LISTEN/NOTIFY

  • Throughput: 1k/s is feasible, but NOTIFY takes a global lock at commit time in the notification queue, so many concurrent writers serialize. It tends to become a bottleneck well before Redis or Kafka would.
  • Payload limit of 8000 bytes; the queue is finite (8GB), and a stalled listener can cause problems for commits.
  • Long-lived listener connections are tied to Postgres connections, which are expensive, and they don't work through transaction-mode PgBouncer.
  • Upside: transactional. The notification is delivered only if the transaction commits. That's valuable if events derive from DB writes, but you've said loss is OK, so you're not paying for it.
  • Adds load to your primary database, which is probably your most precious resource.

Redis Pub/Sub

  • Throughput: 1k/s is trivial (Redis handles 100k+/s).
  • Fire-and-forget, at-most-once: if a subscriber is disconnected, it misses messages. This matches your "occasional loss is fine" requirement.
  • Zero new infrastructure, minimal code, low latency, easy fan-out to many consumers.
  • Downsides: no persistence, no replay, no consumer groups (every subscriber gets every message, so work-sharing across instances needs extra design). Slow subscribers can be disconnected when output buffers overflow.

Kafka

  • Throughput: vastly more than you need.
  • Durable, replayable, partitioned, with consumer groups and ordering guarantees.
  • Cost: a new system to deploy, monitor, upgrade, and learn (brokers, partitions, offsets, possibly KRaft/ZooKeeper, or a managed bill). For 1k/s with loss tolerated, this is substantial overhead for guarantees you've said you don't need.
Why Redis
  • You already run it, so operational cost is near zero.
  • It comfortably covers the load with large headroom.
  • Its weakness (no delivery guarantee) is exactly the tradeoff you've declared acceptable.
  • It keeps notification traffic off Postgres.
Caveats and when to choose differently
  • If multiple instances of a consumer should share work (each notification handled once), plain Pub/Sub will deliver duplicates to each. Use Redis Streams with consumer groups, which give you persistence, acknowledgment, and load-sharing without adding Kafka.
  • If notifications must be tied to DB transactions (e.g., "notify only if the order committed"), LISTEN/NOTIFY is attractive, or use an outbox table that a worker publishes to Redis.
  • If you expect to grow 10-100x, need replay, event sourcing, or multiple independent downstream consumers reading history, that's when Kafka earns its keep.
  • Make sure your Redis isn't set up with settings that make pub/sub fragile (e.g., small client-output-buffer-limit pubsub), and monitor subscriber lag/disconnects.

Bottom line: start with Redis Pub/Sub (or Streams if you want a safety net), and revisit Kafka only if requirements for durability, replay, or scale change.

sample 0

Recommendation: Redis Pub/Sub. It handles 1,000 events/sec trivially, you already run it, and at-most-once delivery matches your loss tolerance.

Postgres LISTEN/NOTIFY

  • Throughput at 1k/sec is feasible, but NOTIFY takes a global lock at commit time, so concurrent writers serialize. It degrades as writer concurrency grows.
  • Payload limit is 8000 bytes. Delivery is lost if the listener is disconnected.
  • Ties notification load to your primary database, which is the thing you least want to stress.
  • Fits only if events originate from transactions and you want delivery tied to commit (no phantom notifications).

Redis Pub/Sub

  • Handles 100k+ msgs/sec on one node; 1k/sec is negligible load.
  • Fire-and-forget: subscribers that are disconnected or slow miss messages (slow ones can be dropped when output buffer limits hit). That is within your stated tolerance.
  • No persistence, replay, or consumer groups. If you later need those, Redis Streams is a drop-in step up on the same infrastructure.
  • Not tied to commit: publish after the DB transaction succeeds, accepting an occasional missed publish.

Kafka

  • Durable, replayable, partitioned, scales far past your load.
  • Costs you a new cluster to run (brokers, KRaft/ZooKeeper, monitoring, upgrades) for guarantees you said you don't need.
  • Justified only if you expect to need replay, multiple independent consumers with their own offsets, or 10-100x growth with durability requirements.

Decision rule: Start with Redis Pub/Sub. Move to Redis Streams if you need persistence or consumer groups. Consider Kafka only if throughput grows by an order of magnitude or event replay becomes a product requirement.

sample 1

Recommendation: Redis Pub/Sub. It handles 1,000 events/sec with headroom, you already run it, and at-most-once delivery matches your tolerance for occasional loss.

Postgres LISTEN/NOTIFY

  • Throughput is fine at 1k/s, but NOTIFY takes a global lock at commit, so concurrent writers serialize. This becomes a bottleneck as write load grows.
  • Payload limit is 8 KB. The notification queue is 8 GB by default, and a stalled listener can make NOTIFY fail.
  • Messages are lost if a listener is disconnected. Not transactional with a queue, so no replay.
  • Adds load to your primary database, the hardest component to scale.
  • Best fit: low-volume signals tied to a transaction (cache invalidation, "row changed").

Redis Pub/Sub

  • Handles 100k+ msgs/sec on a single node, so 1k/s is trivial.
  • Fire-and-forget: subscribers that are disconnected miss messages. No persistence, no ack, no replay.
  • A slow subscriber can get disconnected when its output buffer exceeds client-output-buffer-limit pubsub (default 32 MB hard, 8 MB/60s soft). Monitor this.
  • Zero new infrastructure. Fan-out to many subscribers is built in.
  • If you later need durability, Redis Streams (consumer groups, acks) is a small step up on the same infrastructure.

Kafka

  • Easily handles 1k/s, with durability, replay, partitioned ordering, and consumer groups.
  • You'd be paying for guarantees you said you don't need: a new cluster (or managed service), ops burden, monitoring, and schema/topic management.
  • Justified if events will become a shared backbone (multiple consumers, audit, reprocessing, 10x+ growth).

Decision rules

  • Loss is truly acceptable and consumers are mostly online: Redis Pub/Sub.
  • You want "occasional loss" to mean rare rather than every deploy or restart: use Redis Streams instead of Pub/Sub. Same infrastructure, small cost.
  • Events must originate atomically with a DB write: Postgres (or an outbox table), accepting the load on the primary.
  • You need replay, audit, or many independent consumers: Kafka.

Subscriber restarts and deploys are the main source of loss with Pub/Sub. If your notifications are user-visible and you deploy often, test that rate before committing.

sample 2

Recommend Redis Pub/Sub, unless you need fan-out to offline clients or replay, in which case use Redis Streams (same infra).

Why it fits: 1,000 events/s is trivial for Redis (100k+/s per node). Pub/Sub is at-most-once with no persistence, which matches your "occasional loss is fine" constraint. You already run it, so there is zero new operational cost.

Postgres LISTEN/NOTIFY

  • Works at 1k/s but is the weakest scaling path. NOTIFY takes a global lock at commit time, so concurrent writers serialize; throughput degrades as transaction concurrency rises.
  • Payload limit is 8 KB. Each listener holds a dedicated connection, which conflicts with PgBouncer transaction pooling.
  • Advantage: transactional. The notification fires only if the transaction commits. Pick it only if events originate from DB writes and you want that guarantee, with low volume.
  • Adds load to your primary database, your most expensive and hardest-to-scale component.

Redis Pub/Sub

  • Fire-and-forget: if a subscriber is disconnected, it misses messages. Slow subscribers can be disconnected when their output buffer limit is hit.
  • No consumer groups: every subscriber gets every message, so you can't load-balance work across workers. If you need that, use Streams.
  • Publishing is not tied to a DB transaction, so publish after commit. A crash between commit and publish drops the event, which you've said is acceptable.

Kafka

  • Durable, replayable, partitioned, with consumer groups. All of that is overkill here.
  • Cost: a new cluster (brokers, plus KRaft or ZooKeeper), monitoring, upgrades, and schema/topic management. 1k/s doesn't justify it.
  • Choose it only if you expect 10–100x growth, need replay or audit, or multiple independent consumers need durable history.

Decision rule

  • Fan-out to live connections (WebSocket/push gateway), loss OK → Redis Pub/Sub.
  • Worker queue or need catch-up after disconnect → Redis Streams.
  • Events must be atomic with DB writes, low volume → Postgres NOTIFY (or an outbox table).
  • Need durability, replay, or large growth → Kafka.

anthropic-sonnet crispify
sample 0

Use Redis Pub/Sub. If consumers must catch up after downtime, use Redis Streams instead (same infrastructure, no new system).

Why Redis

  • You already run it, so there's no new operational burden.
  • It keeps notification load off Postgres.
  • Your tolerance for occasional loss removes Kafka's main advantage.
  • It handles 100k+/sec on modest hardware, about 100x your ~1,000 events/sec, with sub-millisecond latency.

Tradeoffs at ~1,000 events/sec

*Redis Pub/Sub*

  • At-most-once, fire-and-forget. A disconnected subscriber misses messages; no persistence or replay.
  • Not transactional with Postgres writes. You can publish for a transaction that later rolls back, or lose an event if the app crashes between commit and publish. Acceptable given your stated loss tolerance.

*Postgres LISTEN/NOTIFY*

  • 1k/sec works, but NOTIFY takes a global lock at commit, so concurrent writers serialize. It becomes a bottleneck as you grow.
  • 8KB payload limit; typically you send an ID and fetch the row.
  • At-most-once, only to connected listeners. A stalled listener can fill the notification queue and eventually make NOTIFY fail for everyone.
  • Each listener needs a dedicated connection, competing with your pool. PgBouncer transaction pooling breaks LISTEN.
  • Upside: transactional. The notification fires only if the transaction commits.
  • Adds load to your primary database, probably your most precious resource.

*Kafka*

  • Far more throughput than you need, plus durability, replay, consumer groups, and per-partition ordering.
  • Cost: a new system to deploy, monitor, upgrade, and understand (brokers, partitions, consumer lag, KRaft/ZooKeeper). At 1k/sec with loss tolerated, you'd pay for guarantees you don't need.

When to choose differently

  • Catch-up after consumer restarts: Redis Streams with consumer groups. Adds persistence, acks, and replay at modest complexity.
  • Events must be atomic with DB transactions: LISTEN/NOTIFY, or better, a transactional outbox table polled or relayed to Redis.
  • 10-100x growth, multiple independent consumers replaying history, or an event log as system of record: Kafka becomes justified then, not now.

Redis operational risks

  • Many slow subscribers can bloat server output buffers. Set client-output-buffer-limit pubsub appropriately and monitor it.
  • On a single instance, failover drops all subscriptions. Ensure clients reconnect and resubscribe automatically.

sample 1

Recommendation: Redis Pub/Sub if every subscriber needs every event (e.g., fanout to WebSocket gateways). Redis Streams with consumer groups if work is split across a worker pool (e.g., push/email senders). Skip Kafka for now. Avoid LISTEN/NOTIFY at this volume unless transactional coupling is your top priority.

Comparison at ~1,000 events/sec

Postgres LISTEN/NOTIFY

  • 1k/sec is feasible, but NOTIFY takes a global lock at commit, serializing commits across the database. This can hurt your primary write workload as volume grows.
  • 8 KB payload limit, so you mostly send IDs and re-query.
  • At-most-once delivery. Listeners need a persistent connection, which is awkward behind PgBouncer in transaction mode.
  • Upside: transactional. The notification fires only if the transaction commits.
  • Risk: puts notification load on your system of record.

Redis Pub/Sub

  • Handles 100k+/sec on modest hardware (~100x headroom), with sub-millisecond latency.
  • At-most-once, fire-and-forget. Disconnected subscribers miss messages, which fits your tolerance for occasional loss.
  • Already in your stack, so no new infrastructure, and client libraries are mature.
  • Downsides: no persistence, no replay, no consumer groups. Every subscriber gets every message, so scaling workers horizontally duplicates work unless you shard channels.

Kafka

  • Throughput far exceeds your need. Durability, replay, consumer groups, and per-partition ordering are excellent.
  • It adds a new system (brokers, KRaft/ZooKeeper, monitoring, upgrades, schema management) to solve problems you've said you don't have. Overkill at 1k/sec with loss tolerated.
Why Redis
  • Loss is acceptable, so Kafka's durability buys nothing.
  • No added load or lock contention on Postgres.
  • Near-zero operational cost.
Caveats
  • Multiple workers sharing load: plain Pub/Sub delivers each event to all of them. Use Redis Streams with consumer groups for load-balanced consumption, acks, and short-term replay, on the same infrastructure. It handles 1k/sec easily.
  • Events from DB writes that must match committed state: write to an outbox table in the same transaction and have a small relay publish to Redis. This gives transactional consistency without NOTIFY.
  • Revisit Kafka if you need: long retention, replay for new consumers, multiple independent downstream consumers at scale, or strict ordering and durability guarantees.

Use Redis Pub/Sub. If subscribers must survive disconnects, use Redis Streams instead (same infrastructure, small API change).

Comparison at ~1,000 events/sec, loss tolerated

Postgres LISTEN/NOTIFY

  • NOTIFY takes a global lock on the notification queue at commit, so concurrent writers serialize. It bottlenecks well before Redis or Kafka would, though 1k/s is feasible.
  • Payload limit is 8000 bytes. The queue is finite (8GB), and a stalled listener can block commits.
  • Each listener holds a long-lived Postgres connection (expensive) and doesn't work through transaction-mode PgBouncer.
  • It adds load to your primary database, probably your most precious resource.
  • Upside: transactional. A notification is delivered only if its transaction commits. You've said loss is OK, so you'd be paying for a guarantee you don't need.

Redis Pub/Sub

  • 1k/s is trivial (Redis handles 100k+/s). Low latency, minimal code, easy fan-out.
  • At-most-once: a disconnected subscriber misses messages, which matches your "occasional loss is fine" requirement.
  • No new infrastructure.
  • Downsides: no persistence, no replay, no consumer groups (every subscriber gets every message, so work-sharing across instances needs extra design). Slow subscribers are disconnected when output buffers overflow.

Kafka

  • Throughput far beyond need. Durable, replayable, partitioned, with consumer groups and ordering.
  • Cost: a new system to deploy, monitor, upgrade, and learn (brokers, partitions, offsets, KRaft/ZooKeeper, or a managed bill). That is heavy overhead for guarantees you've said you don't need.
Why Redis
  • You already run it, so operational cost is near zero.
  • It covers the load with large headroom.
  • Its weakness (no delivery guarantee) is the tradeoff you've declared acceptable.
  • It keeps notification traffic off Postgres.
When to choose differently
  • Multiple consumer instances should share work (each notification handled once): Pub/Sub delivers a copy to each instance. Use Redis Streams with consumer groups for persistence, acks, and load-sharing without Kafka.
  • Notifications must be tied to DB transactions (e.g., notify only if the order committed): use LISTEN/NOTIFY, or an outbox table with a worker that publishes to Redis.
  • Growth of 10-100x, replay, event sourcing, or multiple independent consumers reading history: Kafka earns its keep.
Operational checks
  • Make sure client-output-buffer-limit pubsub isn't set small enough to make pub/sub fragile.
  • Monitor subscriber lag and disconnects.

Revisit Kafka only if durability, replay, or scale requirements change.

s5b

Should we use a monorepo or separate repos? Team of 6 engineers maintaining a web app, a mobile app, and a shared API client library used by both. Give a recommendation.
baseline
sample 0

Recommendation: use a monorepo.

Why it fits your situation

  • The shared API client is the deciding factor. With separate repos, every client change means: publish a package, bump versions in two consumers, and wait for CI in each. In a monorepo, one PR updates the client and both apps atomically, and CI proves nothing broke.
  • Six engineers means low coordination overhead. The usual monorepo pain (ownership conflicts, CI contention, noisy history) mostly appears at much larger scale.
  • Cross-cutting changes are common in your setup. API changes, auth flow changes, and new endpoints typically touch the client plus both apps. Those become one reviewable PR instead of three coordinated ones.
  • Shared tooling is easier. One lint config, one TypeScript config, one set of CI conventions, one dependency policy.

When I'd reconsider

  • The mobile and web teams have very different release cadences *and* need to pin different client versions for long periods.
  • You need strict access control (e.g., contractors who shouldn't see all code).
  • The mobile build toolchain (Xcode, Gradle, Metro) fights with the workspace layout. This is solvable, but it's the most common friction point if mobile is React Native or native.

Practical setup

  • Layout: apps/web, apps/mobile, packages/api-client, plus packages/config for shared lint/TS settings if useful.
  • Tooling (JS/TS): pnpm workspaces plus Turborepo (or Nx if you want more features). Both give you task caching and "only build/test what changed."
  • CI: use affected-only builds and path filters so a web-only change doesn't trigger mobile builds. Cache aggressively.
  • Versioning: the client is consumed via workspace reference, so no publishing is needed internally. Add Changesets only if you later publish externally.
  • Mobile releases: keep separate deploy pipelines per app, triggered by path or tag. A monorepo doesn't mean a unified release.
  • Ownership: use CODEOWNERS so people still get appropriate review routing.

Main risk to manage

Mobile tooling with workspaces (especially React Native and Metro with symlinks) can need extra config. Do a short spike, a day or two, getting the mobile app to build against the workspace client before committing to the migration.

If the spike works, the monorepo will save you meaningful time every week. If it doesn't, separate repos with a published client package is the fallback, but I'd try the monorepo first.

If you tell me the stack (React Native vs. native, JS vs. other languages), I can tailor the layout and tooling.

Recommendation: use a monorepo.

Why it fits your situation

  • The shared API client is the deciding factor. With separate repos, every client change means: publish a version, bump it in two consumers, and wait for two PRs. In a monorepo, one PR changes the library and both apps, and CI proves nothing broke.
  • Six engineers is small enough that the usual monorepo downsides (build times, ownership boundaries, tooling load) won't bite for a long time.
  • Atomic changes. API contract changes, client updates, and UI changes ship together, which removes version-skew bugs between web and mobile.
  • Less overhead. One CI config, one set of lint/TS/formatting rules, one dependency policy, one place to search and review.

When separate repos would win

  • Different teams with different release cadences and strict access boundaries
  • The client library is consumed by external third parties and needs independent versioning and publishing
  • The mobile build toolchain fights with the workspace setup (this is the most common real-world pain point, especially React Native with Metro)

None of these seem to apply to you, but check the last one before committing.

Practical setup

  • Layout:
  apps/web
  apps/mobile
  packages/api-client
  packages/config   (shared eslint/tsconfig, optional)
  • Tooling (JS/TS assumed): pnpm workspaces plus Turborepo or Nx for task caching and affected-only builds. Turborepo is lighter; Nx is more featureful. Pick Turborepo unless you want generators and enforced module boundaries.
  • CI: run only affected projects (turbo run test --filter=...[origin/main]) and use remote caching so builds stay fast.
  • Ownership: use CODEOWNERS so web and mobile changes still get the right reviewers.
  • Releases: deploy web and mobile independently from path-filtered pipelines. Mobile releases are gated by app store review anyway, so don't try to couple them. If you ever publish the client externally, Changesets handles versioning for that package alone.

Pitfalls to avoid

  • Don't let packages import each other's internals. Enforce public entry points.
  • Keep the mobile app's dependency resolution in mind (hoisting issues with Metro and native modules). Test this in a spike first.
  • Don't migrate everything at once. Move the client library and one app first, then the other, preserving git history with git subtree or git filter-repo.

Recommendation: use a monorepo.

Why it fits your situation

  • The shared API client is the deciding factor. With separate repos, every client change means: publish a version, bump it in two consumers, and wait for CI in each. That overhead is the usual source of drift, "works on web but not mobile" bugs, and stale pinned versions. In a monorepo, one PR changes the client and both consumers and CI proves they still work together.
  • Six engineers is a good size. You're small enough that tooling costs stay low and everyone likely touches more than one of the three projects anyway.
  • Atomic changes. API contract changes, client updates, and UI changes land together and are reviewed together.
  • One set of conventions. Lint, TypeScript config, formatting, CI, and dependency versions are defined once.

When I'd choose differently

  • Separate teams with different release cadences, access restrictions, or contractors who shouldn't see all the code.
  • The mobile app is native (Swift/Kotlin) and shares no tooling with the JS/TS side. The shared-client benefit shrinks, though it still works if the client is generated from an OpenAPI spec.
  • You need to open-source or externally distribute the client library independently. You can still publish it from a monorepo, though.

How to set it up

  • Layout:
   apps/web
   apps/mobile
   packages/api-client
  • Workspaces: pnpm workspaces (or npm/yarn) so apps consume api-client by local link, with no publishing needed.
  • Task runner with caching: Turborepo or Nx. Use affected/filtered builds so a web-only change doesn't rebuild mobile.
  • CI: path-based filtering plus remote caching, so pipelines stay fast as the repo grows.
  • CODEOWNERS: assign ownership per directory so review responsibility stays clear.
  • Mobile releases: keep the app's release process independent (tags or release branches per app). Monorepo doesn't mean everything ships together.
  • Versioning: if you ever need to publish the client externally, add Changesets. Otherwise, skip versioning internally.

  • Metro (React Native) can be finicky with monorepos. It needs config for symlinks and watch folders. It's solvable, but budget half a day.
  • CI discipline matters. Without caching and affected-only builds, CI slows down as the repo grows.
  • Don't over-engineer. Start with pnpm + Turborepo. You don't need Bazel or Nx's full feature set at this size.

Use a monorepo.

Why it fits your setup:

  • The shared API client is the deciding factor. With separate repos, every client change needs a publish, a version bump in two consumers, and a window where web and mobile run different versions. In a monorepo it's one PR, one CI run, and both apps are verified against the change before merge.
  • At 6 engineers, the usual monorepo costs (CI scale, ownership boundaries, tooling build-out) are small. The coordination cost of three repos is not.
  • Atomic refactors across API client, web, and mobile become possible. Breaking changes get fixed in the same PR that introduces them.

What it costs:

  • You need path-based CI so a web-only change doesn't build mobile. Turborepo, Nx, or Bazel handle this; Turborepo or Nx is enough at your size.
  • Mobile tooling (Metro, Gradle, Xcode) needs config to resolve workspace packages. React Native is the known pain point here. Budget a few days up front.
  • Release cadences differ: mobile ships through app store review, web ships continuously. Handle this with per-app release tags and branches, not separate repos.

Layout:

apps/web
apps/mobile
packages/api-client

Use pnpm or yarn workspaces, with the apps depending on packages/api-client via workspace:*.

When to choose separate repos instead: if the API client is consumed by external teams or published publicly, or if web and mobile are owned by different teams with different access requirements. Neither applies to you. If the client later goes public, you can publish it from the monorepo without splitting.

sample 0

Use a monorepo.

Why

  • The shared API client decides it. With separate repos, each client change means publishing a package, bumping versions in two consumers, and waiting on CI in each. In a monorepo, one PR updates the client and both apps atomically, and CI verifies nothing broke.
  • Six engineers means low coordination cost. Ownership conflicts, CI contention, and noisy history mostly appear at much larger scale.
  • Cross-cutting changes are common. API, auth flow, and new-endpoint changes touch the client plus both apps. Each becomes one reviewable PR instead of three coordinated ones.
  • Shared tooling. One lint config, one TS config, one set of CI conventions, one dependency policy.

Reconsider if

  • Mobile and web have very different release cadences *and* need to pin different client versions for long periods.
  • You need strict access control (e.g., contractors who shouldn't see all code).
  • The mobile toolchain (Xcode, Gradle, Metro) fights the workspace layout. Solvable, but the most common friction point for React Native or native.

Setup

  • Layout: apps/web, apps/mobile, packages/api-client, optionally packages/config for shared lint/TS settings.
  • Tooling (JS/TS): pnpm workspaces plus Turborepo (or Nx for more features). Both provide task caching and changed-only build/test.
  • CI: affected-only builds and path filters so web-only changes don't trigger mobile builds. Cache aggressively.
  • Versioning: the client is consumed by workspace reference, so no internal publishing. Add Changesets only if you later publish externally.
  • Mobile releases: separate deploy pipelines per app, triggered by path or tag. A monorepo doesn't require a unified release.
  • Ownership: CODEOWNERS for review routing.

Main risk

Mobile tooling with workspaces, especially React Native and Metro with symlinks, can need extra config. Before committing, spend 1–2 days getting the mobile app to build against the workspace client.

If the spike works, the monorepo saves meaningful time weekly. If not, fall back to separate repos with a published client package.

Tell me your stack (React Native vs. native, JS vs. other languages) and I'll tailor the layout and tooling.

sample 1

Use a monorepo.

Why

  • Shared API client is the deciding factor. With separate repos, each client change means publishing a version, bumping it in two consumers, and waiting on two PRs. In a monorepo, one PR changes the library and both apps, and CI verifies nothing broke.
  • Six engineers is small enough that build times, ownership boundaries, and tooling load won't bite for a long time.
  • Atomic changes. API contract, client, and UI changes ship together, removing web/mobile version-skew bugs.
  • Less overhead. One CI config, one lint/TS/formatting ruleset, one dependency policy, one place to search and review.

When separate repos win

  • Teams with different release cadences and strict access boundaries.
  • The client library is consumed by external third parties and needs independent versioning and publishing.
  • The mobile toolchain fights the workspace setup (the most common real-world pain, especially React Native with Metro).

None seem to apply to you, but check the last one before committing.

Setup

  • Layout:
  apps/web
  apps/mobile
  packages/api-client
  packages/config   (shared eslint/tsconfig, optional)
  • Tooling (assuming JS/TS): pnpm workspaces plus Turborepo or Nx for task caching and affected-only builds. Turborepo is lighter; Nx is more featureful. Pick Turborepo unless you want generators and enforced module boundaries.
  • CI: run only affected projects (turbo run test --filter=...[origin/main]) with remote caching.
  • Ownership: CODEOWNERS routes web and mobile changes to the right reviewers.
  • Releases: deploy web and mobile independently from path-filtered pipelines. App store review gates mobile anyway, so don't couple them. If you later publish the client externally, Changesets can version that package alone.

Pitfalls

  • Don't let packages import each other's internals; enforce public entry points.
  • Watch dependency hoisting with Metro and native modules. Test in a spike first.
  • Migrate incrementally: move the client library and one app first, then the other, preserving history with git subtree or git filter-repo.

Next step: spend a day on a spike getting the mobile app to build inside the workspace. If it works, the rest is straightforward. If it's painful, tell me your stack and I'll suggest workarounds or a hybrid (web + client in a monorepo, mobile separate).

sample 2

Use a monorepo.

Why

  • Shared API client. With separate repos, every client change means publishing a version, bumping it in two consumers, and waiting on CI in each. That causes drift, "works on web but not mobile" bugs, and stale pinned versions. In a monorepo, one PR changes the client and both consumers, and CI proves they work together.
  • Team size. Six engineers keeps tooling cost low, and most of them likely touch more than one of the three projects.
  • Atomic changes. API contract, client, and UI changes land and get reviewed together.
  • One set of conventions. Lint, TypeScript config, formatting, CI, and dependency versions are defined once.

When I'd choose differently

  • Separate teams with different release cadences, access restrictions, or contractors who shouldn't see all the code.
  • Native mobile (Swift/Kotlin) with no shared tooling with the JS/TS side. The shared-client benefit shrinks, though it still works if the client is generated from an OpenAPI spec.
  • The client must be open-sourced or distributed externally on its own. You can still publish it from a monorepo.

Setup

  • Layout: apps/web, apps/mobile, packages/api-client.
  • Workspaces: pnpm (or npm/yarn) workspaces, so apps consume api-client by local link with no publishing.
  • Task runner: Turborepo or Nx with caching. Use affected/filtered builds so a web-only change doesn't rebuild mobile.
  • CI: path-based filtering plus remote caching.
  • CODEOWNERS: assign ownership per directory.
  • Mobile releases: keep them independent (tags or release branches per app). A monorepo doesn't mean everything ships together.
  • Versioning: skip it internally. Add Changesets only if you publish the client externally.

Watch-outs

  • Metro (React Native) needs config for symlinks and watch folders. Budget half a day.
  • Without caching and affected-only builds, CI slows as the repo grows.
  • Start with pnpm + Turborepo. Bazel or full Nx is overkill at this size.

Tell me the stack (React Native vs. native, TypeScript or not, how the client is built) and I can sketch the concrete config.
